The case is that of a boy

by Hip Joint Disease for Ni Dr. Williams' Pink Pil

Another extraordinary case—this time the cure of a lad who for Nine years had been a hopeless cripple— has set the public talking of Dr. Williams' Pink Pills for Pale People. Only a few weeks ago there was published the cure of Mr. Kirkland, who had been the victim of Locomotor Ataxy, a disease, terrible not only for the awful agonies it causes,but because ordinary Medical Science admits itself absolutely unable to cure it. Locotnotor Ataxy means, under ordinary treatment a lingering, unspeakably painful Death. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills were proved beyond question to have cured it. This is no empty boast. Absolute and unquestion- able proof from independent authority was given. Dr. Williams' Pink Pills are no quack or patent medicine, but the fruit of a regular doctor's lifelong studies. THE NIGHT

OF THE CRIME.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
FERGUS HOME.

Author of "The Mystery of a Hansom Cab," "The Pagan's Cup," "The Crime of the Crystal," '6Woman—the Sphinx," etc.. ac., etc. CHAPTER XXI

THZ EVENTS OF THE NIGHT.

" What is too late?" asked Will, looking ab Dora.

"My mother's chance of taking advantage of Mr. Pillage's offer."

" Has he made an offer?"

"A very reasonable one," put in Pillage calmly. "Since my wife thinks that I have treated her badly I wish to make amends. I offered to go away for a small annuity, so that she should keep the estate. But, as Miss Tre- mewan truly observes, it is too late now. Mr. Vane is here, and Mrs. Vane will not permit my wife to accept an offer so detrimental to his own interests."

"And I am ruined," wailed Mrs. Tremewan, sitting down with tearful eyes. "I hope you are satisfied, Dora?" All this time Vane had said no word. He remained by the door, his colour coming and going as though he laboured under stress of great emotion, and he fixed his eyes on Pillage in a rather embarrassed manner. As Will had told him that. Pillage was at the Gray House he expressed no surprise at seeing him; but why he should be so nervous, when the appearance of the man was for his benefit, Will could not understand. He looked from Vane to Pillage and back again. But it was the adventurer who first/ spoke. He addressed Paul directly. "Have you seen Mrs. Bedowen?"

"No," answered Vane in a hesitating manner. "1 was waiting to see her, but Hannaford op- peared in her place. I see you have explained yourself," he added, glancing at the weeping Mrs. Tremewan.

Pillage nodded. "My wife quite understands and appreciates the situation," said he cheerfully. "Since I am alive you will come into possession of the property. But since I have been the Deus ex machina to gain you a fortune, I presume you will not refuse to allow me an income." "Walter!" cried Mrs. Tremewa.n in a tone of anguish. "I must look after myself," he said, shame- lessly. "Since you have refused to take ad- vantage of my offer to help you, perhaps Vane will accept my help to place him in possessioh of the rest."

Will laughed grimly. "The name of Pillage suits you admirably," he said. "Quite so," retorted the scamp, 'one of Fate's ironies." __

"We have yet to hear what my cousin says," remarked Dora in a cutting tone; "will you) accept this very handsome offer, Paul?" Stung by her tone of contempt Vane shook his head. "I really do not see that lam called upon to do anything for you, Pillage. Had I not entered so opportunely you would have sold your- self to Mrs. Tremewan, and have kept me out of my rights." "You refuse to do what I ask?"

"I do," replied Vane in a wavering tone, but trying to speak firmly. "You have no claim on me. I shall take possession of the property and give to -Mrs. Tremewan the income you wish for yourself. I have every desire to do what is right." "In that case," said Pillage, quietly, "-I can- not do,,b.etter than follow so admirable an . ex- ample. And to do what is right, Mr. Vane, I must relate the events of the night when your uncle was killed."

"Hat" said Will, in a tone of satisfaction, "now we are coming to the point. I knew Vane was mixed up in the matter." "I have had absolutely nothing to do with it," said Paul, doggedly, and folded his arms. "Waiter!" cried Mrs. Tremewan with a gasp. "Did that man murder my—l mean- Mr. Tre- mewan?"

_ "I have every reason to believe that such is the case, Amy. Will and Dora uttered exclamations, and Mrs. Tremewan dropped back into her seat pale and trembling. But the accused man preserved his composure. • "You have yet to prove that I am the villian you say I am," was his remark.

Pillage plunged directly into his subject and addressed himself rather than to the three spec- tators than to the man he accused. "As I re- lated," he said, ."I had an interview with Mrs. Bedowen at the Eight Bells. She left me shortly after nine o'clock, and I went to bed. But I could not sleep. I mistrusted the woman, as had every reason to do. I thought she might speak to Tremewan about me and get me into trouble."

"It was to her interests not to speak about you to my father." "Quite true, Miss Tremewan," replied Pillage politely, "but the fact is, on that night I was not myself. Ever since Mrs. Bedowen tried to murder me I have had a certain fear of her. However, as I said, I couldn't sleep, so in spite of the dark night and the rain I left the inn and came up the hill to this house." "To see Tremewan?" asked Will. "I hardly know why I came. It was -a kind of idea thit I ought to meet danger half-way, although so far as I knew I was not in danger. My state of mind was inexplicable. Perhaps it was a kind of inkuition that a crime was about to be committed. At all events I secretly left the inn and came to the Gray House. I saw that there was a light in the study. The window was closed, but the shutters were open. This was about a quarter-past ten." "Ah!" said Will, "then you saw me?" "I did. I crept up to the window, and saw you talking to Tremewan. I even managed to hear part of your conversation. Certainly I could not hear very clearly, but it was sufficient to prove that you had spoilt Mrs. Bedowen's game; and I also saw that my chance of getting money was gone, but I was pleased to think that in spite of Vane my wife would be provided for."

"How do you mean, provided for?" asked Mrs. Tremewan. .

"Why," replied her hnsband, "Tremewan as- sured Mr. Hannaford that he would make his will again, and put all question of your inheriting the estate beyond the shadow of a doubt."

"That was why I came down to see him," said Will, with a glance at Vane. "I knew that the will already made might be upset." "And it is upset," said Paul, smiling un- pleasantly. "Thanks to-your having murdered Tremewan," said Pilla.ge, sharply. "That is a lie. I did not murder him."

Pillage shrugged hi s shoulders and went on. "AsJ listened I heard the sound of heavy breath- ing, and I became aware that I had a fellow listener. I saw wmeone on the other side of the window amongst till gorse. Tremewan, inside the room, moved the lamp, and a chance gleam of light sLowed me the face of Vane." "The face of a man you had seen only once!" sneered Paul.

"I have a good memory for faces," replied Pillage, quietly, ".and as you acted the part of a good Samaritan towards me I had particular reason to remember you." "You are certainly repaying my kindness in full," said Paul. "I am only doing what you would do, Mr. Vane. Every man for himself. However, when I saw it was you I crept across, and laid my hand- on your shoulder. You nearly cried out and betrayed yourself, but; I explained who I was, and knowing me to ho an enemy of Tremewan's you were reassured. If you re- member we walked away together on to the high road."

....„.... "And yo'u left me still talking to Tremewan?" asked Will

"Yes. Patience, Mr. Hannaford! I will come to you presently. In the meantime I have to do with Vane here. And von will see that I intend to be very frank about myself. I must ask Miss Trem.ewan to protect me from your violence." said Pillage, smiling. • "Au !" cried Will, with a sudden recollection, "that rope across the road. You—" "Precisely. I looked for Vane in the gorse, as I thought he also might have crept down the road to witness the accident. I could not find him, and after searching for half an hour I de- termined to return to the inn. To do so I had to pass the house, and to my surprise I saw that the window was still open, and the light burning. I crept close, and saw Tremewan.sitting at his desk, with his bead an one side. I looked round, and then stepped inside—l touched him—l found that he was dead. Then I became feagul lest I should get into some trouble, and ran away— like a coward, I confess." "About what time was this?" asked Will.

"Well," said Vane, after a pause ag looking round insolently, "I fail to see how I.can be ac- med of this crime. I did help Pillage with the trap. I got muddy, and my clothes were torn—also my face was scratched. .I did not get back to the Nelson till midnight, and Grant let me in. The next day I heard of the murder, and being afraid, I re- turned to London, hoping that my presence in Dartbridge on that night would be overlooked. And certainly I never thought that anyone would connect me with the murder. Grant let me in when I returned, muddy and torn, and I thought he looked suliciously at me. But that must have been me ly my fancy," "I think it was," replied Will, dryly. "Grant never suspected you, until my father spoke to him about any possible stranger having come to Dart bridge." _ _

"1 don't undetsta.nd." "I mean that my father fancied some stranger might have killed Mr. Tremewan. I am sure he never thought the assassin was the nephew of the victim."

"Nor is he," said Paul, firmly, but extremely pale. "I am absolutely innocent of committing the crime. Although, thanks to my folly, I admit that appearances are against me. I swear that I never knew my uncle was dead until the next morning, when it became common gossip. And then I thought that Pillage had killed him." "If you believed that, why did you not say so?" asked Will. • "Why should I? To accuse Pillage it would have been necessary for me to explain my doings on that night. There was also a chance that Pillage might accuse me—and he has done so." "Because I am certain that I speak the truth," said Pillage, sharply. "It will be no pleasure to me to see you hanged, Mr. Vane. But for my own sake I must be candid. You assuredly left me to see Tremewa.n. I don't say you intended to kill him, but I expect a, row took place, and you lost your head." "Nothing of the sort," cried Vane, vehemently. "I had not motive to kill the man. Do you take me for a fool?" "By no means," said Pillage, ironically. "I take you for a desperate man. Your fortune was at stake, and the sudden death of Tremewan was likely lo make you a rich man. You knew the secret remember. I, on the contrary, had every- thing to lose by the death of your victim. I had only to claim my wife to make things unpleasant; whereas if Trernewa.n learnt your knowledge of the secret, your game was up." "All this is very plausible," replied Vane, folding his arms, but I swear that I am innocent. If I n.m hanged my blood will be on your head. And remember," he proceeded solemnly, "you are murderin the man who fed you when you were hungry, and"— "Bah! That sort of thing does not go down with me. lam not sentimental, my good Vane." "No villain is," retorted Paul, and became silent. He saw that it was hopeless to appeal to this man, and that speaking only made matters worse.

There were a few moments of silence, for every- one was more or less exhausted by the prolonged conversation. Indeed, Mrs. Tremewan now looked thoroughly ill, and could scarcely keep from faint- ing. She staggered to her feet, and waving away Pillage, who would have gone to her assistance, she took her daughter's arm. "Let me go to my room," she said, faintly. "I feel seriously ill. Walter—she looked at her husband—may God forgive you for all the misery you have caused. Later on I will tell you my plans. Paul, I ac- cept your offer of an income, which is what you should do under the circum.stances." "May I be alive to give it," said Paul. gloomily. "You will be. I do not believe that you killed your uncle." She would have said more, but that the effort was too much for her. "Dora, Dora I Take me away." The poor woman was not jesting. Her strength had given way, and it was scarcely to be wondered that it should after the strain to which she had been subjected. Dora would rather have re- mained, as she wished to speak to Will about all that had taken place. But her mother needed her care, and she was forced to go. However, she was well satisfied to leave matters in the hands of her lover. As she passed him, she pressed his , band. ." Thank God you are cleared," she said in/ a low voice, and led her mother out of the mini. When the women were gone Will looked at the two men. He had already made up his mind what to do. "This is a serious matter," he said. "Pillage, are you prepared to come with me to the police- office and make a formal statement?" " Yos," was the reply. "Vane killed Tremewan. I am ready to make an affidavit to that effect." . "Come then," said Will, leading the way to the door.

CHAPTER XXII.

MRS. BEDOWEN'S REVENGE,

Paul Vane returned to his hotel in a mixed frame of mind. He was now satisfied that he would obtain the Tremewan estates without his claim being contested; but at the same time he could not disguise from himself that his liberty was in da.nger. Indeed, seeing that Will and his accuser, Pillage, were even now at the police- office, it was not improbable that he might be arrested before nightfall. As he saw no way of proving his innocence in the face of such an ex- plicit accusation as the adventurer intended to formulate against him, the idea of flying to London crossed his mind. But on his way down the High Street he saw that he was being shadowed by a man,- and remembering Will's in- timation that he was being watched, Ile gave up the idea of flight. To run away now would only result in his earlier arrest.

A- surprise awaited him when he entered his sitting-room. A stout woman was seated beside the fire sipping a glass of port. "I have been waiting for you," said Mrs. Bedowen' with a bland smile, "and I took the liberty of ordering a slight refrePhinent. I don't know you, but as I came to be of use to you I thought I might take such a liberty—if you can call it one." Paul was too sick at heart to object to this im- pertinence. "I expected you before," he said, sitting down heavily. "To be sure, and I should hays come before, but that young Hannaford was in the way. I waited in a shop on the other side of the street trying to make up my mind to come over. You see," said Mrs. Bedowen, with engaging familiarity, "I was afraid of falling into a trap. He has no moral standard I fear—l mean young Hamiaford, though to be sure he is in such danger that all Ins morals have gone to the four winds.' I hope they'll hang him," she finished, vindic- tively. Paul could not help smiling, in spite of his dismal humour. "It is I who am in danger of being hanged," said he. Mrs. Bedowen dropped her biscuit she was nibbling, and sat up with a watchful look in her keen eyes. "You she exclaimed. "Why, in what danger do you and, Mr. Vane?"

" nage"— • " Aye! Aye! Pillage. You have learned that much. Well, sir, I said I could produce Pillage alive, and I have done so. You must now promise me faithfully my annuity. The estates are yours." "For how long," queried Paul, -with a sigh. "Pillage accuses me of having murdered Trerne- sn.n, and I am likely to be arrested. He is at the police-office now along with Hannaford." "Clever," said Mrs. Bedowen, brushing the crymbs off her ample lap. "I always did think Pillage a scourdrel, and Heaven knows I have had enough experience of him. So he accuses you, does he? and the estate, if you are hanged, will go to the Crown. Ha! What about my annuity then, Mr. Vane? I ask you that." "There woul dnot be much chance for your getting it, I fear." "Oh! And Pillage thinks that after all my work I am going to put up with that!" She frowned and folded her arms. "We'll see about. that. I would have spared him. Now since he plays me false I won't." "Spared him! How do you mean?" "That's my secret," she replied, composedly. "I have a good many, haven't I. Lord, you may well say that, Mr. Vane, but my secrets are all paying ones. I did very well out of Tremewa.n. Now I am going to do well out of you." "I can do nothing," said Paul, as he looked at her with a certain amount of hope in his eyes.

Bless you, my dear young man"—Mrs. Bedowen became motherly—"you don't know your own powers. It is well seen you are not used to lots of money. The Tremewan estates are worth, I should say, thrte thousand a year. Quite that. You ask what you can do. Well, it so happens that all Tremewan would give me by way of an annuity was two hundred a year. Make it four, ray good man, make it four." "Why should 1 do that, even if I had the

power?" You have the power. Oh, yes! The estates will borne to you, thanks to me. And you will live to enjoy them, thanks to me again." Paul jumped up much agitated. "Do you mean to say"— - _ _

"That I know who killed Tremewan?" finished Mrs. Bedowen, triumphantly. "Of course I do. I have known it all along, but I kept the secret to myself until I could see a chance of selling it in the best market.. ,You will buy it from me, and go free, a rich man. Four hundred a year!" ended 'the housekeeper, greedily, "and little enough for the present_ of life and fortune that I am making to you. There are pen, ink, and paper over there," she said, rubbing her nose; "a slight, memorandum now . . just a few lines bind- ing yourself to pay me four hundred. Eh?" "But can you really —'" "Just you write to my dictation, and I'll tell you all about it." Dominated by the woman's masterful, manner, Paul went to the writing-table and wrote a few lines, dictated very swiftly. Mrs. Bedowen was a woman who knew the value of time, and in five minutes she had in her pocket an acknow- ledgment from Vane that he would pay her four hundred a year on receipt of certain information. "I'll hare it stamped," she said; "it is well/ to be on the right side.in these things. I know the law. Good!' she slapped her pocket. " Well ! Well !" cried Paul, impatiently,. "Who killed Tremewan?" "Pillage himself." "I knew it—l knew it!" cried the young man, smiting his hands nervously together. "He ad- mitted that he saw Tremewan dead." "But he did not admit that he killed him," finished Mrs. Bedowen. "Quite so. You can't expect a man to cut his own throat. tell you. what I know, and then we'll go to the police- office. Oh!" she clenched her fat •fist, "I have waited many a long year for my revenge on that beast. Now it has come at last. Yet if he had kept faith with me, I would have spared him. I'm not sorry though that he has thrown the game into my hands. I want to see him hanged." Her expression was so malignant that Paul Vane shrank back, his nerves all quivering. He saw what a terrible woman he had to deal with, and his earnest desire at this moment was to get. out of her company as quickly as possible. "Will you explain?" he asked, timidly. "Ha!" she said. unclenching her fist and smil- ing quite amiably, "to be sure. I must earn my income." She slapped her pocket again. "Four hundred a year!" she cried. "Not 90 bad for a. poor weak woman. Better than being dependent on a brute of a man," said Mrs. Bedowen, mapping her fingers, "that for the sex!" • "The story—'

"Well, you may call it rather a sensation • novel," she said. "To begin at the beginning, r, went to bed on that night at ten o'clock. But E saw that Tremewart was ill at ease. Pillage was in Dartbridge, and I did not know whether he was playing me false or not. I determined tc ascertain for myself the cause of my master's un- easiness. I went to bed with the other servants, as I did not want him to think that I suspected anything was wrong One must be circumspect,

"Yes! Yes!" cried Vane, quivering with eagerness. "Go on!' "Don't be in a hurry. Pillage will be arrested. soon. enough. I went to bed, as I said. I even retired' actually for the night, assuring myself that my. fears were unnecessary., But I. could not steeti—l really could not close my eyes. I am not what you would call a sensitive woman," said Mrs. Bedowen, with pride, "and I have no patience with nerves, but my feeling on that night told me, something was about to happen—some- thing dreadful. Perhaps my interview with Pillaze upset me. as it certainly did. However,

the main point is that I could not sleep. Alter eleven o'clock I could bear the state of sus- pense no longer, and put on my dressing-gown to come downstairs. I knew that my master would be in his study, and there I went. The door was closed, but I heard the sound of voices raised in anger. I feared to go in, and resolved to see without being seen. I therefore opened the front door and slipped out." "1 see," .said Paul, eagerly. "You went round to look in at the window?"

"I did. It was a clever idea. The night was !old and rainy, and I was but thinly clad, but I lid not mind that. I was determined to learn what was taking place, and to find out for my- ;elf if Pillage was deceiving me. I had certainly Left him at The Eight Bells, but he might have followed me." "He did—he did. He said as much himself. liforeover I saw him there myself." "Saw him where?" demanded Mrs. Bedowen, with a frown.

"Outside the house watching it. I was there also, as I knew that Hannaford intended to betray me. Pillage met me, and it is for that reason that he says I killed my uncle." "Did you see nothing of me?" asked Mrs. Bedowen, suddenly. "No. How could I? At the time you speak of I was making my way back to this hotel by a circuitous route that I might not be followed. I will tell you all about it when you finish your story."

Mrs. Bedowen sniffed. "You seem to have put yourself in great danger, Mr. Vane. I dare- say if I had not come forward Pillage would have succeeded in getting you hanged. But I stick to my Promise. I will save you at all costs." "It will be just as well," said Paul, a trifle dryly. "If you do not your income will be gone." "Ha! So like a man reminding a woman of benefits conferred.. However, we can talk of that later. As I say, Mr. Vane, I went round and peeped into the window—which to my astonish- ment was open. I saw my master and Pillage conversing furiously—quarrelling violently. I would have rushed in to separate them, but that the thing occurred which I feared. Pillage—as I noticed, had in his hand a cord which I guessed had been taken from the window-curtain—"

"How did you guess that?" asked Vane. "Very easily—from the colour. The cord was twisted blue and red, and the light was clear enough for me to see the tints. He passed be- hind Tremewan with the cord, and threw it over my poor inaster's head. I tried to cry out, but my tongue stuck to the roof of my mouth. I saw Treraewan struggle for a time, and I fell down on the wet grass in a kind of faint. Yet I still retained some of my senses, and heard him rush past. me. He mimed me in the darkness. I entered the room, and found my poor master dead."

"Why did you not give the alarm?" "Because I am not an absolute fool," replied the houselgaper, in a tart tone. "I had been to see Pi life—he had committed the crime: I saw in a flash that if I said anything I should be declared an accomplice, and might get into set-it:as trouble. • No! No! Matters were too com- plicated for me to give the alarm. I resolved to retire to bed and say nothing about the affair. I relocked the front door and retired to bed. The next morning I was down first, and went to the study on the plea of seeing if the housemaid had cleaned it. I then gave the alarm. The rest you know." "And you can swear that you saw Pillage commit the murder?"

"Certainly. Come to the police-court—or office, whatever it is, at once, and I'll make my affidavit." "But even at this late hour you may be ac- cused of being his accomplice. There is danger for you."

M-rs. Bedowen snapped her fingers again. "A woman who has to earn an income of four hundred a year does not fear danger," she said, with dis- dain: " Leave the matter to me! Now that the whole story of my connection with Tremewan is known lam bound to be unpopular. A trifle more or less on account of the murder won't hurt me."

"Of course, you know that all your blackmailing business will come out in court," said Paul. "Bless the man, don't I say that it will! I'll tell the judge all about it myself. So long as I can't get into trouble—and I don't see how the law can seize me—l don't care two straws. Of course lam running a risk. I don't deny that, but I want the money. You leave things to me, as I say, and I'll get out of the mess all right. I have dealt with policemen and lawyers and judges before now."

There was something terrible in the shameless way in which the woman spoke. Paul knew well enough that she might get into serious trouble— she might even be accused of being, an accessory after the fact. Yet the danger did not seem to give her the slightest uneasiness. She rose, ar- ranged her flamboyant bonnet in the mirror over the fireplace, and expressed herself ready to go. Vane did not much care about walking up the High Street with so conspicuous a female, but there was no .help for it, so he submitted to his destiny. After all Mrs. Bedowen was saving his life. If it had not been for her he could not have proved his innocence. Vane gave a shudder as he thought how he had for some time stood under the shadow of the gallows. He was a highly- strung young man, with a keen dread of pain. But he did not mind inflicting it on other people. "When Pillage is sentenced you won't walk down the street with me," was Mrs. Bedowen's remark as they left the hotel. "I expect all the pious people will be making mouths at me. I shall be called Mrs. Judas"—she here laughed heartily at the idea. "I am afraid I, slvell have to give up the idea of taking boarders at Sakombe. People are so mealy-mouthed. Just as if most of them would not do what I am doing under the same provocation. However, I'll go to London. I can live very well on my income there, and I'll be able to enjoy life. It's rather late to begin— still there is a kick or two kit in me yet, Mr. Vane." _

- -:-6--an you enjoy life after all the misery you have caused?" _ _

"Oh, Lord!" Mrs. Bedowen stopped and sur- veyed her companion with great contempt. "Are you going to preach? A nice parson you are. LONDON TRAMWAY REFORM.

Highly enthusiastic was the reception of the -Prince and Princess of Wales in South London When they went to open the new electric trapa- Way system from Westminster to Tooting via liennington and Clapham. These trams have long been eagerly awaited by the dwellers south of the river; and, despite the enormous outlay involved, the London County Council felt themselves justified in carrying out the scheme themselves. The Prince and Princess of Wales were , accompanied by their elder children, and journeyed from Westminster to Tooting and back on a tramcar, receiving a great ovation all along the line from the huge crowds which had gathered to do honour to the occasion. Mr. J. W. Benn, who is chair- man of the Highways Committee of the L.C.C. travelled on the car with their Royal High- nesses and took their fares. Each member of the. royal party handed him a halfpenny, oblivious that the full 'fare was threepence, and in return he solemnly punched and handed them tickets as mementoes. In his speech Mr. Benn christened the tramcars the "poor man's motors," a happy phrase which is likely to stick. " Poor men's motors," however, are no better behaved than those of rich men, and on Saturday. when the cars were open so the public, there were two breakdowns. An excellent new system for controlling the queue has been introduced with the new trains, which are sure to be very crowded.

A NAVAL MASTERPIECE.

The new battleship Commonwealth, built for the British Government, and just launched from the Fairfield Yard, Govan, is one of a series of the largest battleships in the world. She is the largest ever built on the Clyde, and has been constructed in ten months, which constitutes a world's record in naval construc- tion. The requirements of the Admiralty are now extremely stringent, as they require that battleships built by private firms shall be ready for commission after leaving the builders' yard. The draught of these huge battleships is so great, and the Clyde so shallow, that there was some doubt of the possi- bility of a Clyde firm fulfilling the Admiralty services required of them in future. It is to be hoped that the Clyde trustees will take serious

THE BREAKER OF THE MOTOR MILE RECORD.

Considerable interest attaches to the most recent feat of Mr. Alexander Winton, the well-known American snotor driver, as he will be one of the three representatives of the States in the Gordon-Bennet Cup contest. He has set up a new record across the Atlan- tic of z minute 2 seconds, and he is expected to take a very leading place in Ireland, as he 2S a magnificent "driver of great nerve and has a fine car. The world of motors is just now very active and full of life, as on Sunday begins the great Paris to Madrid rare, the former of the season's international events. This will occupy some three days, and be run

------,....

MR. ALEX. WINTON ON HIS RECORD-BREAKING CAR.

in stages owing to the great distance, and it is -expected close on 300 cars will start! The roads ih France are in perfect condition, but the same cannot be said of those on the other side of the Pyrenees. In July comes the Gordon- Bennett Cup, and it seems as though Ireland will be very full for the great event. So little chance is there of getting accommodation in the neighbourhood owing to everything having been booked up, that the Automobile Club is making arrangements to set up tents for their members. Both these races are of Particular interest owing to the International flavour, and England is making every effort to hold the place she won last year.

PRESTON'S NEW M.P.

Mr. John Kerr, who succeeds the late Mr. iianbury as member for Preston, is of Scottish Parentage. He is 5o years of age and was educated at St. Andrews and Glasgow Univer- sities. He is an engineer, and has been all his business life associated with the profession, more especially in connection with the build- ing and equipment of tramways. He is the Principal member of the firm of Dick, Kerr nnd Co., of Kilmarnock and Preston, in which latter, town the firm has founded during the last five years large electrical equipment and tram works, employing at the present time between 1,500 and 2,000 men. Mr. Kerr is a magistrate for East Lothian, is a sportsman,

Fairfield Yard has earned renown and the envy of builders in all countries by building the Commonwealth in the reCord time of ten months. Her con- struction has gone on so quietly that she has been launched with two birds' nests in her hold. The Commonwealth will have a dis- placement of 16.350 tons. She is 425 ft. long, with 78 ft. beam, and a draught of 261. Her indicated h.p. is to be 18,000, with a speed of 18.5 knots, A feature of the ship will be that her ten 6 irr: guns are to be pro- tected by an armoured battery in place of casemates, Her armalmnt will consist of four 9.2 in. guns and 24 smaller guns.

THE NEW TECHNICAL COLLEGE FOR GLASGOW.

One of the chief features of the King's most Successful and popular visit to Scotland was his day in Glasgow, where he received a rous- ing reception. Arriving at Queen Street Station their Alajeities drove straight to the site of the New Technical College, which was the osten- sible object df their visit: The building will stand at the corner of George Street and St. John Street, and there great stands had been erected to aCcomodate • five thousand people. It was raining in torrents; but this did not damp the enthusiam of the loyal onlookers. The King laid the foundation stone with the usual ceremony, which was followed by an

WILL LOOK.

Ippreciatlve speech, thanking the city /or its loyalty and its reception. As a memorial the King was presented with a silver trowel with a handle of ivory and gold, an exceptionally large and fine ivory mallet, • fifteen inches in circumference, and a level and plumb of gold, all suitably inscribed. After the ceremony had been concluded their Majesties visited the magnificent Municipal -r3uildings, the New Art Galleries, the University, .and other places of interest, leaving Glasgow from Maryhill Station shortly alter five in the evening, and returning to Dalkeith. The visit appeared to give pleasure on every side, and not least of all to the King and Queen themselves.

THE TROUBLE IN MOROCCO.

The civil war in Morocco, or rather the revolt of the Sultan's brother from his authority, is not attracting t4e attention it deserves in Great Britain, for the Moors are living in stirring times. Despite its proximity to Europe. Morocco is one of the least civilised countries in the world, and one of the most fanatical even amongst Mohammedans. There are prac • tically no roads, and the whole country is in- fested by wild tribes, who live by plunder. Morocco City and Fez are the chief towns, but Tangier is the best known, as many European visitors pass the winter there. The present trouble has recently centred round the typical

TETUAN.

Moorish town of Tetuan, which. is forty miles from Tangier by road, and forty-eight miles by sea from Gibraltar. It had 'been practically in a state of siege for some time, as the Kabyles had concentrated their forces round it; but towards the end of last week it was relieved by reinforcements, sent by the Sultan by sea on a British steamer, who inflected a crushing defeat upon the rebels. This may help to clear up the situation: but if not, European intervention is talked of as possible. It is stated' from Gibraltar that the Spanish Government has thrown up defences at Ceuta, where six heavy suns will be mounted.

TRAINING COLLEGE BAZAAR. The Dean of Manchester's Humour.

TOTAL PROCEEDS.

On Saturday afternoon the Dean of Manchester re- opened the bazaar held in the Parr Hall, Warriaqton, with the object of liquidating a debt on the practising schools in connection with the Training College and raising a fund to transform Orford Hall into a hostel for the accommodation of more students. The Rev, Morley Stevenson presided andlin the course of his observations confessed that on the first day they were& little bit disappointed with the results. A far larger number of people attended on Friday and in the evening the room was very full, a good deal of vigour and energy being shown on all sides. He had every reason to believe that that would be very much more the case that day, in spite of the inclemency of the weather. He would like to explain that although the sums they had taken by the actual sale of goods had not been so large as they might have wished, those amounts by no means represented the measure of interest which was being felt in the cause. He thought he ought to tell them that they had a subscription list which amounted to over £9oo—(applause)—and various gifts of money which had been sent in by kind friends who had not been able to attend the bazaar, would materially swell the total receipts. They were not therefore entirely dependent upon the sums realized at the bazaar. Although that was the third day of the bazaarthey did not intend to give away the things which had not been sold, nor did they intend to reduce to any great extent. They were by no means at the end of their resources and they had other means which be would not mention of disposing of all the goods which were left on the stalls. In asking the Dean of .IVanches- ter to re-open the bazaar, the Principal said he took a very deep and sincere interest in the welfare of the college and the institutioi owed a debt of gratitude to him which it could never re-pay: (Applause). The DEAN of Manchester was in a facetious mood. It was, he said, an act of supererogation to open a bazaar. He thought it was one of the pieces of cant of the present day that no bazaar coulabe considered to be open unless someone came and told people who walked in atthe opm door that the bazaarwietopen.(Eaughter).Allniling to the Principal's statement with regard to the subscriptions he had received. the Dean said he never divulged matters of that kind, lie was running a particular fund at pre- sent in Manchester, but be never told a single creature how much he had in the exehequer, (Laughter.) He found he alwayswanted something more. Of course he would publish a balance-sheet at the end. and some people would be surprised to find that so much money had been collected. (Laughter.) Speaking of the object-of the bazaar he referred to the necessity which existed for the provision of further. means of educating those who were to educate the children. If there was one thing more than another which sat close to his 'heart it was that they should spread their net as wide as they could, and show those who were not altogether in sympathy with them that the Church of England was in the very forefront of the educational system of the country, particularly in the matter of training their students for. teaching. (Applause.) They wanted to live up to their past; they wanted to justify their present it 7.; well as they felt they could certainly justify their past. In wishing success to the bazaar he said he did ant know why a similar effort on a small scale should not have been made in the three cities with which the Training College was connected—Chester, Man- chester, and Liverpool. He was quite sure Warrington would do its best. Hehad every confidence in Warrington; he admired Warrington—(applause)— at a respectful distance. (Laughter.) He thought, to use a vulgar expression, that it took the cake in the narrowness of its streets. There was a very strange mixture in Warrington. It seemed to have one foot in the past and was trying to make- an effort to put a foot into the present and the future. (Laughter.) It had got its work before it,and had a great deal to do before it mended its ways. (Laughter.) He wished God- speed to the undertaking and hoped at the close of the bazaar they Would be able to contribute a handsome sum to the object they had in view. (Applause.) On the motion of the Rey. E. C. E. CARLETON, seconded by Mr. ATKIN, a hearty vote of thanks was accorded to the Dean, and the sale of work was resumed. The receipts for the three days were :—Thursday, £l4O (is, 11d. ; Friday, £lO2 18s, 5d.; and Saturday, £164 Cs. Bd., making a total of £407 12s.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

A DRUNK EN'HUSBAND.-WIFE OBTAINS A SEPARATION ORDER.

At the Warrington Police Court, on Friday, 31ary Mills, 13, Catherine-street, applied for a separation order ^gainst her husband on the around that he Wa 3 an habitual drunkard.—Complainant stated that her husband had been of drunken habits for I)bout thirteen years and was scarcely ever sober. He suffered from cleliriun tremens nearly every week,and in consequence of his threats when in drink she was afraid to live with him.—Defendant contended that the facts were not as bad as stated by his wife.—The magistrates granted a separation order with gl perweek alimony.

TUE Colonial S—aere' MR. CHAMBERLAIN retary has once more AS HOME RULER. become a centre of

interest and miner' tainty. A glowing light is thrown on the jacig- merit which has been formed of his character 11; other countries by the simultaneous declaration° certain German and American newspapers, that u:3 intends to overthrow Mr. BA.LFOUR and usurp 1315 place. And just at the moment when these thing, are being said there appears Mr. JusTrif MoCARTirY's book. In a baker's dozen of leading Parliamentarians, of course, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN" if; a prominent figure, and Mr. McCARTiir has some' thing to say about him which is not new t° politicians, which has never before been so plainlY revealed to the public at large. The illumination thrown on the Colonial Secretary's past by tbe , brilliant historian and novelist who was for ti while the leader of the Nationalist party in the House of Commons, will go far to explain tho distrust, not limited to our own country, which assumes that Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is always on the watch to forward his own interests at the ex- pens° of his colleagues. "I regarded him as one of the truest English friends the Home Rule cause had ever made," writes Mr. McCAßniy. This refers to the period just before the general elee' tion of 1885. Mr. CHAMBERLAIN was alwaYe emphatic in his declarations that if the large majority of the Irish people desired Home Rale their desire should be granted to them by the Imperial Parliament. Mr.CHAMBERLAIN was then expecting to be made Irish Secretary. He and Sir CHARLES DILKE had planned a visit to Ireland to study the pleblern of Home Rule on the spot, but that visit was never paid. it was Lord FREDERICK CAVENDISH who became Irish Secre tary in succession to Mr. FORSTER, and Afr' CHAMBERLAIN abandoned his Home Rule opinione to take up a position of savage and unrelenting hostility to Mr.Grabsro.Nes proposals. To anyone who is disposed to argue that the cruel murder of Lord FREDERICK CAVENDISH is a sufficient el- planation of this change of view,it is merely neces- saryzto quote Mr. MoataTEY's statement that gr. CHAMBERLAIN was still ready to become Irish Secretary after the murder had taken place. (34 Mr. GLADSTONE'S reasons for not gratifying this ambition we shall probably hear when Mr. IVIOIL' LEY'S biography is published. But what we surrniee is that the great statesman relied on Mr. CRAW BERLAIN'S absolute faithfulness at that time, and believed that he would be satisfied with whatever position was assignel to him ny his chief. After Mr. FORSTER'S resignation the present Colonial Secretary was so convinced of being appointed tn succeed him that he held a conference withia th° precincts of the House of Commons with certain of the leading Nationalist members, who under" stood clearly from him, says Mr. McCAnTriy, tb if he went to _lreland in the capacity of Mini; Secretary he would go as a Home Ruler,and woulu expect their co-operation and assistance. Th° historian finds the subsequent change of opinin° inexplicable. "His conversation," he says, accomplished with a suddenness that seemed te, belong to the days when miracles were yet wore upon the earth." But, after all, is there anythin,g inexplicable about it? Ode of the greatest reforms which has been Tfrected in Workhouses during recent years has 'peen the abolition of pauper nursing. It is not 741,„.anY years since the nursing of the sick in the warrington Workhouse hospital was left largely in the hands of paupers, but to the credit of the W arrington Board of Guardians they took Immediate advantage of the Local Government toard order, abolishing pauper nursing. An effi- tient stall of trained -nurses now undertakes the rPrsing of the sick poor at the Workhouse *hospital and some of the beneficial results were /eferred to at the meeting of the Guardians on Wednesday. One result has been a partial re- Inoval ot the prejudice of poor people against enter- g the Union infirmary. The Master reported that there were 44 more cases in the hospital compared with the corresponding period last year. At first Sight it may appear that the more efficient nursing 14 the sick poor has thrown an additional burden °II the ratepayer but this is rather a short-sighted law to take of the matter. It may be taken for ranted:that the pattents will recover, more speAily :r,,ora their illness in a well-equipped hospital utin if they were allowed to lie for weeks in their homes, which in many cases are little better ,Luan hovels. By speedily restoring these people '4O health and enabling them to follow their em- 'PloYment the Guardians are performing a work 'which in the long run will do more to make these People independent of poor law relief than was Possible under the old bad system, which made the Poor dread the thought of being a patient in a Workhouse hospital.

The sad story of the suicide of Arthur Shrews- 'bury, the great Notts cricketer, illustrates the manner in which sensitive natures are preyed upon 0Y fancy. Shrewsbury had been suffering from a disease which, if his medical advisers are to be credited, was by no means incurable; but he 'became afflicted with hypochondria, arising from the conviction that his malady was permanent. Ills case presents an interesting, if melancholy, study. Probably no man who ever appeared on the cricket field was under more perfect control than Shrewsbury. Cool in the most trying circum- stances, undaunted even when there was no hope for his side, he was the best type of the sportsman 'who plays for the game and plays his level best no znatter what the test. Yet this strong man, who eculd deny himself the luxuries in which other men indulged, who never allowed himself to be Carried away by the excitement of the moment,or to be taken off hisi,guard,became the:prey of a mor- .l3!d fear which led to the sad tragedy that ended fils life. Strong minds are sometimes weakest When they dwell upon affairs of self; and the 3teat batsman was always a sensitive man. Yet In the end he showed the same determination that ad characterised his career on the cricket fist(' ,the manner of his suicide. Acts like these are to contemplate, hard to explain, and the only Q,Dinreent which can fittingly be made is the 'lnzent, "Oh, the pity of it!"

RUNCORN AND WIDNES.

FATAL ACCIDENT TO A RIINCORNIAN.

_ On Sunday information was received in Runcorn that John Pye, licensee of the Shqmrock Inn, Regent-street, had been killed in a street accident at Dublin on Satur- day' Deceased was a bricklayer by trade and followed that employment while his wife managed the public- house. On Monday Mrs. Pye went to Dublin and brought the body to Runcorn on Wednesday morning. Deceased leaves six children.

611-A-R-IT-17--60N-CERT IN AID OF A RUNCORN WORKMAN.

On Saturd•ty and Sunday concarts ware held for the benefit of Mr. Jamas Gibson who ins bean prevented from following his employment owing to a fracture of the thigh sustained when the Ship Canal was being constructed. At one time he was in the Volunteers and was cornet soloist in the Widnes Subscription band. The audiences unfortunately were small there only being about £2 clear, despite the free Services of Professor Miller and Mr. Shaw, jun. . AN UNRULY YOUTH SENT TO TRU' "CLIO."

At the Runcorn Petty. Sessions an order was made sending John Meredith Evans, a boy of twelve, to the training ship "Clio" until the age of sixteen years.—Tt was stated by Mr. Lightburn, who appeared for the School Board, that the boy was absolutely unmanage- able. When remonstrated with he would throw his clogs at his mother. His father absconded in March last year and his whereabouts could not be traced. It was decided as his mother Ellen Evans, supported herself and two other children by washing, not to ask for an order to contribute towards her son's maintenance. A DRUNKEN OCTOGENARIAN AT RUNCORN.

At the —litineC:in Police Court, on Thursday, Mary Ellen M`Cormack, who said she was eighty-six years of age, was charged with being drunk and incapable in Church-street at eleven o'clock on Tuesday night.— Police-constable Davies saidhe found the prisoner lying across the footpath helplessly drunk. He took her to a lodging-house, but the proprietor would not receiveher, and he was obliged to take her into custody.—The old woman promised to go to her son at Warrington,and she was discharged. ALLEGED NEGLECT OF CHILDREN.

At the -Runcorn Petty Sessions before Messrs. J. Ockleston (in the chair), and A. R. Norman, on Monday Albert Ravenscroft, Wyvern Place, was summoned for neglecting his four children, under the age of sixteen years, in such a manner as to cause them unnecessary suffering and harm.—On behalf of the N.S.P.C.C. Mr. Knowles prosecuted and proved that since the 13th November last year Inspector Hackett had had the case under observation. The defendant had been repeatedly warned but he took no notice. His hose was beastly dirty and repeated visits showed thelt was neither fire, food, nor clothing in the house. Defendattt was a very lazy man and stayed in bed until the afternoon. For the last few weeks he had only given his wife Gs. per week.—The case was adjourned for a month to givd, the defendant another chance. Sr. PAUL'S GLILD. _

Mr. Moulton hoped that every member of the association would con- sider it a duty to become a subscriber to these deserv- ing charities. ' CA STNEK-KELLNER ALKALI COMPANY.

The seventh annual general meeting was held on Wed- nesday, at Cannon-street Hotel, London. Sir W. Mather M.P., who presided, moved the adoption of the report andthe declaration of a dividend at the rate of six per cent. per annum for the half-year ended March 31st. He stated that the board were pleased, considering the con- dition of thealliali trade during the year, to be able to pAsent so favourable a report. Bleaching powder was the greatest of the company's products; and they had a manufacturing process far less costly than any other. For two or three years an arrange- ment was in force between the large manufac- turers to maintain a fairly remunerative profit, but this had, dining the year, ceased to exist, and the profit on bleaching powder had considerably diminished. The board had continued to develop the processes which were identified with the company's name, but they saw in the future important improvements tending towards still greater ecomony. The profits were slightly less than those of the previous year. The directors had considered it right to utilize the reserve depreciation fund as capital to put down plant of a character in accordance with the scientific development of the times and thereby to cheapen and improve all the products which were made by the company. They were trying experiments continually in the treatment of many sub- stances which had hitherto not been treated electrolyti- cally. While they desired to pay as high a dividend as they could, yet they could not be parties to withdrawing from the industry the necessary support which it re- quired out of the revenueprofitsto maintain and enlarge its operations in the future—Mr. E Hardcastle second- ed the motion which was adopted.

EARLEiTOWN CO-OPERATIVE SOCIETY.

The quarterly meeting of this Society was held in the Co-operative Hall on Monday evening, when there was a large attendance of members. Mr. D. Forrester presided. The report and balance sheet stated that the cash receipts were £13,217 19s. Bd., being an increase over last quarter of £269 Os. 2,14., and compared with the corresponding period last year, showed a decrease of £l2l 15s. id. The net prolltis realized were £1,865 10s. 9d.. which it was proposed to dispose of by paying a dividend of 2s. 10d. in the .£ to members, and is. sd. in the £to non-members, absorbing £1,85145. 5d., and the balance £l4 Gs. 4d. to be added to the Contingent Fund. During the quarter 108 new members had been admitted, 58 had been struck off or withdrawn, leaving 2,000 on the register, 1,705 of whom have had dividend warrants made out. Messrs. T. Caine and E. Jones were re-appointed on the Committee of Managetnent. The usual grant of 7s. 6d. per head was made to the employes trip. The question of making a small charge for the delivery by van,'of parcels to members was dis- cussed, but it was decided to make no move in this direction at present.

At the outset, Mr. &SRN CRITCHLEY stated that it had been customary when the President was not at the meeting for the Vice-President to take the chair. lie (Mr. Critchley) was the vice-president, but he was unfor- tunately unwell and not fit to take the chair. As the appointment has been carried by\a, unanimous vote of the meeting you cannot get beyond the fact. The CHAIRMAN said Mr. Clive had been given to understand that he must devote whatever time was required to the supervision of the affairs of the society. Mr. POTTER: That _is kery_ vague.

The CRAIEtNIAN: The legal adviser informs me that Mr. Clive is giving up his present position in order to attend to his duties.

THE PROSPECTS OF THE SOCIETY.

The CHAIRMAN said that perhaps at this point they would want to hear something as to the future working of the Society, and as to how the Society was likely to benefit the shareholders. (Hear, hear.) Although the stock of their association had dropped to such a great extent he thought that those members who had patience to hold their shares as they were, would do well in the future. He found on going through the accounts that they had had very serious losses, and the loss they had had, or anything like it, could not possibly occur again. The society would make a net profit of something like £1,290 or £1,300 a year. Con- sidering that in the past the profits of the society had been even larger than the amounts 'tamed it was terrible to thinktthat they were placed in such a posi- tion. In as much as there would be the profit of £l2OO or £l3OO a year from their undertakings he suggested that those people who had given notice of withdrawals and felt inclined to hold them as such should receive each year a certain amo !nt towards the repayment of their withdrawals. He also suggested that the 1,200 should be divided into two parts, and the £6OO he set aside for a sinking fund and the remaining £6OO be de- voted to paying oft those who had given notice of ' withdrawals. He did not mean that the first person on the list should receive the whole of his money but that £6OO should be divided among a reasonable number of:shareholders according to rotation. At the end of two years there would be a fund of £1,200 to be

COLONEL PILKINGTON M.P. AT EARLESTOWN.

The annual meeting of the Earlestown and District Conservative Club was held on Tuesday evening. Mr. T. J. Gillespie presidedand he wassupported by Messrs C. li. F. Borrow,W. Hollhnd Owen,J. Robinson and Colonel Pilkington M. P. The usual business of the club was transacted the only item of importance being the resignations of the Secretary, Mr. T. Haselden, the Treasurer, Mr. Thorpe, and the Chairman of Committee Mr. Catterall. Eaeh had held office for a number of years,and Mr. Gillespie paida eulogistic tribute to their services. Mr. C. H. Lea was appointed Secretary, Mr. T. Bowes treasurer.

Colonel Pilkini,vton 'then gave an address, in which he said he thought it his duty inParliament to move the rejection of a Bill on Friday, which he considered was a Bill very much out of place brought forward in the wrong way and by the wrong people. The Bill he referred to was ‘a new Mines Regulation Act coming from private members. Manyof the clauses in the Bill to which he objected were very harmful. All collieries had their rules, which had been made under the auspices of the Home Secretary and Inspectors, but the Bill in question proposed to entirely revolutionise these. It proposed to form a Board of three which could sweep away all the rules which had been for years got. to- gether. It also wanted to interfere with the check- weighman,it wanted to enlarge his position. Why, he asked, did they want to enlarge his position at all? The check-weighman was instituted so that the employer's record could be checked. The miners had a right to .appoint a check-weighman whose duty would be to check the weight impartially and completely. He was not the master's servant, and that was all he had got to do, and if they gave him anything more to do, they woald entirely spoil his ability to perform that Impor- tant function. Another important point in the Bill was a most disastrous thing, or would have been for the coal trade. This was the provision that for every mile of pit there must be an extra pair of pits, but it would not increase the ventilation to do this. The relief pits would not wind coal at all, but an enormous amount of extra capital would be needed to provide them. He then went on to refer to the licensing question,chietly on the point of compensation for licences taken away, and alluding to the policy of Mr. Chamberlain as outlined in his recent speech at Birmingham on the question of preferential tariffs, he expressed himself • as entirely in accord with the view of the Colonial Secretary.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0074.txt | included=true | included =====
NOTES BY THE WAY AT EARLESTOWN.

It is stated that there is to be a slight reduction in rates for this district. It is only a penny in the pound but slight as the relief is, it will be very welcome. So far as the district rates are concerned the burden is not very heavy, but according to all accounts the Guardians intend to make up for any deficiency on the part of the Council. At the present time the Poor rate is quite high enough being -2s. in the pound, but the Guardians are vivocious, and they intend to have their pound of flesh.

Already they have put threepence in the pound on for Education purposes, although it has been decided not to bring the Act into operation until September 'next. This looks very much like albit of sharp practice and the only explanation one can get is that the Guardians want to get some money in advance. It is a grand thing to be a ratepayer, especially when you are asked to pay for something you don't get value for. I should think it was time enough to put the rate on when the Educa- tion Act had been adopted.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0075.txt | included=true | included =====
CRICKET.

The miserable conditions which prevailed, on Saturday put an end to any idea of Cricket. Both Earlestown and Newton had-important fixtures, but they had to be abandoned. On Wednesday however,Newton completed their fixture with Wigan on the ground of the latter, and assisted by Miller of Earlestown and Huddles. ton, late of the old Trafford ground staff succeeded in gaining the victory. On Saturday the local rivals Newton and Earles- town will have a trial of strength on the ground of the latter. It is a good fe..v years since the clubs have met owing to differences, but they appear to have buried the hatchet, and on Saturday they will compete for superiority. There is sure to be a large gathering of the supporters of both clubs and it is hoped the contest will be marked by the greatest friendliness on both sides.

TIIE EDITCATION BILL.

Mr. R. Potts, clerk to the Cheshire County Council wrote enclosing a list of the voluntary schools in the Rural district pointing out that the County Council were required to appoint a manager of each voluntary school and requesting that the Council would furnish him with the names and addresses of more than one person for each school mentioned therein. The manager to be appointed by the County Council of each. school would be in addition to• the manager appointed by the Minor Local Authority Parish Council or Parish meeting as the case might be. It was decided that the School Attendance Com- mittee deal with the matter, and that they in each instance consult the places named on the enclosed list, which have not yet been directly represented by the County Council.

AN INSANITARY TIOCSE AT LA.TCHFORD.

Two letters were received from Mr, W. L. Bonner, of Latchford, complaining of the insanitary condition of the drains at is house in Fairfield-road. The Inspector reported that he had seen the drains, and had rectified the matter.

THE TIiELWALL NUISANCE AGAIN. Admiral Jones-Parry writing from Boscombe, Hants, wad he hoped to be present at the meeting tint day. He did not take the reply to Mr. collier as reported on the 29th March as an answer to his question. The Pro- fessor's report made the question more a muddle than ever. He asked certain questions regarding the Thel- wall nuisance, which he required answering. On the letter being read an altercation ensued between the Chairman and Admiral Parry,the Chairman emphatically stating that he could on no account allow a discussion on the Thelwall nuisance so long as the County Council had not communicated their report to the Rural Council on the last report forwarded them. On the motion of Messrs. Darlington and Edwards it was resolved that the matter be not re-opened.

A PERPLEXED COUNCIL. On the consideration of the Highway Committee's minutes, Mr. Collier moved that the portion referring to the appointment of the Highways visiting Committee be deleted. _ _

Mr. Spruce seconded, contending that the appoint- ment of the Visiting Committee had been cut and dried, a proceeding he did not agree with. Mr. Darlington objected. ' The proposition was, however, carrieil. Mr. Collier then moved as an amendment that two committees be appointed one for Budworth district and one for the Frodsham district. The former committee to consist of Messrs. Mclntyre' Pilling, Knowles, Bradford, Darlington, Mountield, Mawdsley, Woodward, and Baxter. The Frodsham committee to consist of MeSsrs, Guest, Stokes, Baxter, Woodward, • Linaker, Lewis, Wright, Pilling and Darlington. The amendment to the Highway Committee's minute being carried by eleven votes to seven, a discussion of the scheme ensued in which the Chairman pointed out the impracticability of the scheme in which the committees would be looking after their own districts by probably pursuing totally different policies. The amendment being then pnt as the substantiative motion was lost by a large majority. After further discussion during which the ' clerk pointed out that there was now no Highways Visiting Committee which would throw back the work of the Highway Department a month the difficulty was solved by Mr. Pilling moving the adoption of the original minute which had been rejected. The minute was accordingly approved.

The Monthly meeting of the Warrington Rural Dis- trict Council was held at Bewsey Chambers on Wednes- day afternoon under the presidency of Mr. T. lialton. There xverealso present :—Messrs. Brooks, ail!, Mather, Knowles, Houghton, Backhouse, and Dr. Murray with Dr. Sefton, (medical officer of health), Messrs. J. C. Sutton (clerk), S. Sutton (assistant clerk), F. SM ALL-PDX.

The CLERK informed the Council that he had received a letter from Dr. Sargent, County Medical Ciincer of Health, calling attention to the increase in the number of cases Of small-pox in the county andenclosing copies of circular giving information in regard to the disease. Happily up to the present there hati'been no small-pox cases in the district and hehoped this state of affairs would continue.

'LONGFORD DEPOT. THE COUNCIL ASKED TO TAKE ACTION. CORRESPONDENCE WITH THE TOWN CLERK

The CLERK said he had received the followin peti- tion to which 107 signatures 'were appended :—" We, the undersigned beiry, property civners and occupiers in the parish of Winwia with liuline and Orford, do hereby pray your Council (who constitute the district sanitary authority) to take some immediate steps to have the nuisance caused by the manufacture and manipulation of the excreta from the borough of Warrington by the Warrington Corporation their works at Longford removed. This nuisance is so great that it is intolerable and we, the inhabitants, are suffering daily from its effects. It is evident that the nuisance is a growing one, and will increase as the popular of the borough increases. We the petitioners consider that it is time some action should be takes by your Council to have this nuisance abated."

The Clerk stated that a gentleman called upon him (Mr. Sutton) a few days ago to say that the people who lived about the neighbourhood were complaining that they could not sleep at night as they "blew off" some- thing from this particular place. From what he stated the people appeared to be in a very poor way. Mr. .NOWLES said that -between eight and nine o'clock at night when the wind was blowing in the direction of Winwick-road the stench was double what it was in the day time.

The CLERK, continuing, said the Council were aware that one movement in the way of calling upon the Cor- .poration to do something to alleviate at least the terrible effects which were experienced from the smells and so on at Longford was to summon the Corporation under the Manufacturing Acts, whatever other course the Council might take under any othersection or authority of the law. A summons was taken out, but they found there was a difficulty in .bringing the case onat the next sessions as the County Medical Officer MIS goingabroad. Messrs. Arthur Browne and Crompton wrote the Town Clerk on behalf of the Council and received the follow- ing reply on April 28th :—Referring to your letter of the 28th March last, which has been duly considered by the Sanitary Work ,Committee, T am instructed to inform you that, with the view of minimising the emis- sion of offensive vapours from the Works at Lorigford, they have recently purchased and are now erecting additional machinery at very considerable cost. The Corporation have also called man expert in the treatment of sewage matter who has for sometime been engaged in experiments for dealing with the vapours and waste waters at the works. These experiments have proved successful, so much so fhat the inventor has patented the process but as they were conducted on a small scale the Corporation be voted the Sum of £3OO for further ex- perimental tests to be made. The contract with the inventor has been preferred, and it is hoped that the furtherexperiments will be forthwith made. If they should again prove successful about whicb there if little i doubt it s the intention of the corporation to put down a permanent plant. I may assure you that every- thing will be done to prevent any cause of complaint and I trust that your clients having regard to the diffibulties with which the (gorporation beset and the nature of the manufactory carried on will refrain from taking any course which would disturb the friendly relations which have so long existed between the two authorities.

To this letter Messrs. Arthur Browne and Crompton replied stating that after considering the matter their clients had instructed them to inform the Corporation that under the circumstances related by the Town Clerk in his letter they would withold taking proceedings until the next meeting of the Rural District Coune ii provided they were informed in the meantime as to what progress, if, any, had been made with the intended mprovement. On May 18th the Town Clerk wrote beg to inform you that at the last meeting of the Council a resolution was agreed to for construction of a culvert, from the concentrators for the purpose of drawing off the vapours to be cremated at an estimated cost of £l7O. Mr. Russell Duncan, the sewage expert, is coming to Warrington on Wednesday next, for the purpose of commencing the erection of the special plant for treat- ing vapours and waste water. I have no doubt that when the new plant has been put into operation there will be little or no cause for complaint. Mr. BALL thought it would be satisfactory to leave the mutter in abeyance until the next meeting. Mr. KiOWLES hgead, but rem irked that some years age_the Corporation promised to spend £2,000 with the object of abating the nuisance. He understood that if no action was taken the petition which had been read would be forwarded to the Local Government Board. The Council decided to take no further action for a month.

_OFFICIALS SALARIES INCREASED.

... . In accordance with notice of motion, Mr. K:cow LES proposed the re-appointment of Dr. Sephton, (medical officer of health), and Mr. F. Kerfoot (nuisance inspec- tor) at the increased salaries of £l5O each (Mr. Kerfoot to be supplied with uniform). Mr. BROOKS seconded.

The CLERK said he had been in correspondence with the Local Government Board in regard to the re-appointments, and the Board had Written stating that fn the event of the Council Agreeing to the re-appoint- ments they would raise no objection. The motion was agreed to. •

The CLERK said it was the custom with all Councils occasionally to revise the salaries paid to the staff, particularly if the work of any deparament had grown and the responsibilities of the officials were increased. Reasonable spaces of time were always permitted to elapse between each revision, the object being not to have implications for increases coming in at uncertain times but that the whole of the payments of officers might be considered together in relation to the work they had to undertake. lt was only fair to say that in regard to the highway department of that Council, the books showed that the work was increasing materially. Tee Clerk submitted details in regard to the books. Mr. MATHER asked if instead of Mr. Newcombe having to attend to the books in the surveyor's depart- ment an experienced gentleman could be engaged to do that work alone, so that the Snrueyor might simply superintend the outside work.

Sir. KNOWLES said there were already two assistant clerks as well as an 'assistant surveyor. The accounts were only an index of the work done.

Mr. MA.THEu then proposed that Mr. N wcombe's salary be increased from £1.60 to £2OO per annum. All he need say was that the Surveyor deserved theadvance. The resolution was unanimously agreed to. On the proposition of Mr. KNOWLES, seconded byMr. BROOKS, Frank Reginald Bickford wts appointed an assistant in the Surveyor's Department with wages at the rate of six shillings per week. OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

Cairo-street Sunday School Anniversary

'Pais annual event took place on Sunday and was as successful as its predecessors. The preacher, morning and evening was the Rev. W. H. Drummond, B.A. of Belfast, formerly minister at Cairo street. There were large congregations, many availing themselves of the opportunity of renewing their acquaintance with Mr. Drummond. At the morning service he spoke upon the words" Come follow me. and I will make you fishers of men," urging self surrender to Christoand at the evening service be based his discourse on the text. "He that is mighty hath magnified me." An anthem was effici- ently sung at each service by the choir. Mr. James Muir, A.R.C.0., presided at the organ. There was a scholar's service in the afternoon, conducted by Mr. Joseph Owen, of Warrington, a former superintendent of the Sunday school. The Sunday school choir assis- tld in the singing, which was of a hearty character. Mr. Muir presided at the organ. The service was also taken part in by the Rev. W. H. Drummond, who was Afterwards entertained to tea in the Congregational Parlour by the teachers and members of the congrega- tion. About 70 persons sat down. Mr. T. Birchall, J.P., made a few remarks in which he expressed the pleasure of those present in seeing Mr. Drummond amongst them once more. Mr. Drummond also spoke and alluded to the pleasant time he spent amongst them as their pastor. The collections throughout the day realised about £4O.

Open Meeting of the WycEffe P.S.A. Society.

WARRINGTON EDUCATION COMMITTEE. The Proposed Staff. MR. F. MONKS RESIGNS the CHAIRMANSHIP. The Salary of the Director of Education.

There was a full attendance at the meeting of the Education Committee, held on Monday evening. Mr. F. Monks, J.P., presided. FILLING UP VACANCIES.

The CHAIRMAN read a letter from the Town Clerk, which stated that Alderman Shaw Green had ceased to be a member of the Council and also a member of the Education Committee. At a meeting of the Council Councillor Brophy was elected, while Mr. Wm. Bibby was appointed a co-optative member of the committee. Councillor Wilkinson was appointed a member of the Finance Committee and Mr. Bibby a member of the School Attendance Committee.

IMPORTANT RECOMMENDATIONS. CLASSIFICATION OF TEACHERS.

The School Management Committee submitted some important recommendations with reference to the scale , of salaries for teachers.

THE COOKERY CLASSES._

It was recommended that when a scale of salaries for teachers is adopted, it shall be made retrospective to Ist April, 1903; that the cookery mistress be engaged to devote her whole time to the cookery classes at a salary of .£lOO per annum, and that an assistant be ap- pointed at a salary of ..£7O per annum; that the engage- ment of all pupil teachers begin on July Ist ; that the necessary text books be lent to the pupil teachers attending the institute from schools under the com- mittee, and that the pupil teachers be allowed to keep the books, provided a satisfactory report is received from the teachers of their classes after the yearly examinations; that each head teacher be allowed £1 to meet petty cash payments, land that an account of the expenditure of the same be kept to be audited by the Borough Treasurer; that each head teacher wake a return of the approximate amount of stationery, &c., required during the year; that each head, teacher of the school In which fees are charged keep a record of the fees received.

APPOINTMENT OF TEACHERS. At a meeting on the 7th May, it was resolved that consent be given to the appointment of the following 'teachers :—Mr. Gilbert Lewis, Mr. A. E. Sproston, and Mr.P. S. Rothvrell, Warrington Plrochial, o£9o each (provisionally) ; Miss Edwards, Latchford National Infants' School, £75; Miss E. A. Clare, Latchford, St. James' (mixed), £47 10s. A resolution of the Finance Committee with reference to the salaries of Mr. A. Green, Warrington Parochial Schools, and Miss S. Jones, Trinity National School, for the month of April having been reacl,it was resolved that the kalaries of these teachers be paid for the month of April 1903 ; also that in the absence of exceptional circumstances, in cases of illness,the full salary be paid for the first month, and half the salary for the second month, that a teacher is absent from school; that the head teacher of each school record the absence of all teachers, and the reason thereof on the monthly attend- ance returns; that all the schools be closed on Thurs- day, May 21st, 1903 ; that a summer course of nature study be held; that Mr. A. Deane beappointed teacher at a salary of 7s. per lesson, and that the fee to students be 2s. 6d.
