JOLLEY pointed out that the sub-committee I did not arrive at the decisidn without very careful , deliberation. Though the teaching profession was an honourable profession and the members of it diseharged their duties in an admirable manner as a whole, there were black sheep in every flock and the regulation re- ferred to was provided in order that any dereliction of duty might come in proper form before the School Management Committee month by month, and if neces- sary, be reported to the full:committee. He submitted it would be an extremely regrettable step if the amend- ment proposed were carried. He hoped the committee would not begin by being carried away by sentiment. There must be no shirking of plain facts being brought forward when necessary. Mr. THOMAS MAY pointed out that in Government official life there was generally a book kept to record the hour of arrival and departure, and seeing that teachers would virtually become public officials under the present management, a record should be kept of their arrival and departura, and of leave of absence. It was impossible to carry on the work efficiently unless 1 they had such a record. Councillor Upsos, in answer to Mr. Jolley, pointed out that the haul teacher suffered most through the absence of his staff, and he would not tolerate absence on frivolous grounds. (Hear, hear.) If any teacher absented himself or herself without sufficient reason, the head teacher would send the name of such teacher to the School Management Committee. In answer to Mr. May, he might say a record was kept for Government inspection, and the Inspector went through the logbook kept by the headmaster and noticed all the absences. Councillor Upson's amendment was then carried by a large majority. PRACTICAL COOKERY WASTED. Alderman SMETHURST thought, with regard to the cookery classes minute, that they passed a resolutionat the last meeting to have a head mistress at 10s. per lecture. Councillor W. ROBERTS: Wasn't it dressmaking? (Laughter.) Alderman S3IETHURST said he had been under the im- pression that it was cookery. But in reference to cookery he was sure they were all anxious that their girls should be taught how to cook—(hear, hear),—and what to cook. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) He hoped the cookery mistresses would teach their girls how to cook at the least cost, and not to endeavour to teach them things that were utterly impossible for a working- man to provide. It might be all right for those who kept spices and a dozen or two of eggs in their cup- boards —(laughter)—but that was not the kind of cookery teaching they wanted. They wanted classes where pupils could be taught to cook beefsteak and porridge, etc., in the workingman's style, and if there were alot of bones left teach them h,e, to make a dinner with them. (Laughter.) Let them have something practical and not something out of the reach of poor people. (Hear, hear.) Councillor PlErtPorce: I think after this cookery lecture from Alderman Smethurst it would be &graceful act on the part of the Council if we could appoint him sub-inspector 'of the classes, and I suggest that. (Laughter.)

Councillor ROGERS: I second that. The Rev. JOHN YONGE remarked that although Alder- man Smethurst's speech had created a great deal of B,a-inset-no:it, there was a good deal of sense about it, and from what he (Mr. Yonge) had heard from ladies in town the teachers in cookery might give more hints on the subject. They wanted. their girls to learn common- Bence every-day cookery, and not something ornamental as would grace the tables at the Mayor's banquet. It was necessary for the girls to be taught something that would be appetising to the workingman when he oame home. (Hear, hear). The CHAIRMAN observed that working men's cookery and sick cooke.ry were taught. The Rev. JOHN YONGE said ho was glad to hear that, but Alderman Smethurst's humorous remarks should be considered to have a grain of sense in them. THE TRAINING OF PUPIL TEACHERS. Mr. R. T. FAIRCLODGH thought they should be safe- guarded against pupil teachers, who had been taught at a groat cost to the town, leaving as soon as they were taught. If they did not continue in the profession, the commtttee should be reimbursed. They should also consider what proportion of pupil teachers they had for the scholars that were being taught. Alderman MONKS said it seemed to him a great pity that the members of the committee did not show their practical interest in the work by going to classes and seeing what was actually being done. If they had done that not one word would have been said that had been said that evening, and instead of criticising they would come there to praise the work that was being done. All along since cookery instruction had been given under the auspices of that committee it had had special refer- ence to what the children were likely to require in their own homes. The fees which the scholars earned would pay the expenses of carrying on the classes. With re- gard to what Mr. Fairclough had said, the details in the organization had yet to be considered, and the matter would be brought forward in time. There were some schools which had not had pupil teachers. That was not fair to the other schools who had to train pupil teachers. The number of teachers to scholars was part of the organization plan which would be brought for- ward in the future. Mr. .FAIRCLOUGH said he presumed they would be safeguarded. Alderman MONKS said they could not do that. They would have to comply with the rules and regulations laid down by the Government department with reference to pupil teachers. There was a tendency to stop pupil teachers from teaching in the schools. The minutes, as amended, were then adopted. MINUTES TO BE SUPPLIED TO THE PRESS. COUNCILLOR W. ROBERTS AND THE FOUNDA- TION MANAGERS. At a me3ting of the General Purposes Committee it was resolved that Mr. F. Monks beappointed Chairman of this committee for the ensuing year; that the minutes of the committees shall not be supplied to the press before they have been approved by the Education Committee; that a sub-committee consisting of the chairmen of all the committees consider and report upon the applications for the transfer to the Local Authority of the following :—(a). Litchford Natioaal Infants' Department. (h). St. George's Mission Infants' School. (c). Warrington British School: Infants' Department, Thewlis-street, Infante Depart- ment, Cairo-street. Conacillor DODDS moved an amendment that the minutes be confirmed with the exception of the paragraph ,stating that the minutes should not be supplied to the press before they had been approved by the Education Committee. He did net think it *as desirable to make such a condition. Mr. A. J. JOLLEY seconded. He said he was present at the meeting of the General Purposes Committee and strenuously opposed the resolution. He wished to say in the most emphatic manner possible that the resolu- tion seemed neither desirable nr practicable. Even if it were desirable he ventured to think it would be absolutely impracticable. At the first meeting of the committee it was unanimously agreed that the public and

the press should be admittedand the present resolution seamed to be entirely reactionary. Councillor BENNETT supported the amendment, and said he was entirely opposed to the resolution at the committee meeting. It would really be non-effective even if it were allowed to stand. He thought it was in every way desirable that they should take the public into their confidence. When questions of serious importance had to be discussed, it might be of considerable help to know what the public thought of the policy to be adop- ted. Whilst he was the last man in the world to be moved by any gust of popular opinion, or anything of that kind, they must remember that they were there as the servants of the public, and they ought to take the public into their confidence and get to know their views on any important question. Councillor W. RouErtTs said he happened to be the unfortunate individual who proposed the resolution which was carried by a maiority of one vote. He had not the same reason for proposing it now as he had at the committee meeting. It, was proposed as a protest against the publishing of the appointment of foundation managers before the names had received the sanction of

Continuing ' he said they would admit that it was necessary to have a staff to control and minage the affairs of the commit- tee. Mr. Murray had very carefully considered what was necessary and he submitted recommendations re- quiring a staff of about six for the work he had to do. ;His (the chairman's) own feeling after his business ex- perience of the past forty odd years was that they would not be sufficient to do the work. At any rate, if the staff should prove to be large enough his feeling i was that the new officials would perform their duties I much more efficiently, that most clerical staffs in the !various works. Mr. Monks proceeded to oompare War- rington with other towns in which' School Boards had been in operation. In Salford where there were 19 schools and 11,774 scholars they had a staff of 10 for purely elementary education. In Oldham where there were 19 schools and 11,122 scholars the staff numbered ten. In Warrington there was a larger number of schools and children than were being attended to by any School Board in Lancashire, the schools numbering 21 I and the children 13,593. They were very anxious that the work of organization should bens complete and per. fect as possible. What they had to do-was, to a large i extent, to evolve order out of chaos. The twenty one , schools which,the authority had to take over had been managed by various committees, and in addition they ! had technical and secondary education and the pupil , teachers' centre. and the continuation schools. They I felt that the work of the Director would be arduous and , he would need all the gupport he could possibly get from 'an efficient and well-trained staff. If, to begin with, Mr. Murray had to train his own staff it would throw additional work upon him which he (the chairman) was quite certain he would not be able to perform

Mr.gonks detailed the various duties of the new officials and went on to refer to the proposed increase of Mr. Murray's salary. He pointed out that the Director had to take charge of the elementary, secon- dary, and technical education, as well as the pupil I teachers' centre. He had obtained figures with refer- ence to the salaries paid in nine Lancashire and York- shire towns with School Boards, and all of them were ; forelementary education only. Manchester and Liver- pool, of course had always a larger number of schools and scholars than Warrington, arid he believed that the I salary of the Director in Manchester had been raised to ; £l,OOO, whereas in Liverpool it was certainly not less than £BOO. Galborna-Street Baptist Church. Plain Speaking on Poverty.

The above Church was crowded in all parts on Sunday evening last, when the Pastor delivered the third of a series of sermons on several questions. After replying to several questions relating to previous sermons, Mr. Donnie° proceeded to discuss the question of poverty, with special reference to the books published by Roam- tree and Booth. No one who lifted his eyes from his own private life would fail to see, said the preacher, that the present age was saturated with the desire for social amelioration. Workmen with grimy hands and women with eager eyes were subordinating all other issures to the social one, so there was no need to apologise - for in- trod*ing that question there and then. When they were confronted with the fact that at least 25 per cent. of the population of England were living inn state of poverty he thought they would agree.thatsome solution was urgently needed. When he said that 25 per cent. of the population of England were living in a state of poverty he meant that 25 persons out of every 100 ware living in a lower state of comfort and nutriment than that provided for our paupers in our workhouses.. Taey must admit that drunkenness, betting, carelessness &c., were important factors in producing poverty yet in the vast majority of cases irregularity and uncertainty of income were partially if not wholly responsible, for as long as a man could not with the utmost tool earn sufficient to keep body and ueul together in comfort, vice and drink would play ravages amongst the people. The result of this condi- tion of affairs resulted in overcrowding and underfeed- ing with their on 'voidable results. Overcrowding re- united in a high death rate especiallyamong the children and the result was that we were as a nation standing on the verge of the calamity waich has befallen France —a stagnant population. Underfeeding resulted in the physical deterioration of the people and a vast amount of miserable and dragging ill-health. If they doubted his statement let them examine the army returns and they would find that. out of 83,0)0 recruits who presented themselves to the reeruiting sergeants 24,000 were rejected as physically unfit although their army requirements were ludicruousl!„- low. In .concluding. Mr. Dunnice made an earnest appeal. for greater national earnestness. To-day we treated our horses in a more humane ratnner that we treated our poor and paid better attention to our criminals that we did to thousands of our honest workingmen. Surely it was not too much to ask that every honest worker should be as warmly clad as healthily housed and as regularly fed as our criminal convict. To bring this about they must send mten o Parliament not to gratify their ambitions bat to save the people and they must elect men on. their town councils not because they had axes of their own to grind but who were distinguished by honourable service.--Next Sunday in the absence of the pastor who is preaching for Dr. Aked, at Liverpool the Rev..lohn Davies will occupy the pulpit. -- ' STOP A COUGH IN ONE NIGHT. WARRINGTON SHOP ASSISTANTS.

The Operations of the Local Branch of the

tlational Union.

Business and entertainment were agreeably combined on Thursday last week,when a represeatative assembly of members of the local branch oT the National Amalgamated Union of Shop Assistants, Warehousemen and Clerks, met at the " Hop Pole " Inn, Horsemarket- street. The proceedings took the form of a smoking concert, but opportunity was taken of dealing with various matters of importance to the branch. Mr. Heaps, chairman of the branch, presided, and the attendance included the energetic secretary (Mr. E. Whittaker.) At the outset The CHAIRMAN said it was very encouraging to know that since the visit of Mr. Pearce, the official organiser last month, no fewer than twenty new members had been enrolled. This spoke much for the energy of the Warrington branch. It had been thought desirable to • present a petition to the boot dealers and clothiers ' with the object of introducing them to close at 7. 30 and thus come into-line with other businesses in the town. They commenced to canvass the boot dealers on Monday night and with two exceptions they were all agreeable to closing their shops at the thin desired, to commence on the first Monday after Walking Day. Up to the present, they had not can- vassed any of the clothiers but he did not suppose they would experience any-difficulty with them. The Chair- man alluded to the Wire Weavers' dispute and said the secretary thought it advisable to remind the members that the workmen were still out on strike. At the last meeting but one of the branch it was decided to levy a penny per month on each member, to assist the wire weavers but only three shillings had been received. That did not say much for the generosity of the meal- . bars. The Shop Assistants' 'Union was allied with all the other Trade Unions and if they had any dispute in any branch of their trade and appealed to any of the Unions he had no doubt there would be a liberal response. He thought the least they could do was to . contribute a penny per month on behalf of the wire weavers. He had received acircular from the Secretary to the Trades and Labour Council setting forth the cir- cumstances of the dispute and stating that collecting sheets had been issued. An enjoyable musical programme was then commenced, songs being rendered in capital style by Messrs. J. Owen Birkhead, Sawyer, and Pickersgill: Mr. Birkhead sup- plied the pianoforte accompaniments. During an interval, the Chairman delivered a short speech and reminded, the members that they must be alive to their own interests as a class. He referred to the Shops Bill drawn up by the Union and introduced by Sir Charles W. Dilke and also to Lord Avebury' Bill. Tao latter Bill Aid not contain ono definate clause,and if it were passedthe shop assiejantewould be in a worse position than they now occupied. He urged them to take an interest in public affairs and pointed out that they could be a strong force in municipal and parliamentary elections if they would exert themselves to obtain a vote and then return the men who would Serve them faithfully. The vote Was the one machine whereby they could obtain the rights they were asking for, and if they sent the right men to the Town Council and the Shops Bill became law they would see that no byelaws were framed which would be detrimental to the shop assistants. If they returned Labour members to Parliament they would support any 111Mstlres which might be introduced in favour of shop assistants. (Applause.) Mr. E. WitiTTAKER said that if Lord Avebury'sllill became law the shop assistants would be as far behind as they were fifty years ago. At the meeting of the Trades Council on Tuesday a resolution was passed in favour of Sir Charles Dilke's Bill and coudemiling Lord Avebury's measure. He proposed "That this branch welcomes the introduction of the Shops Bill in the House of Commons by Sir Charles W, Mike, as a reasonable and practical measure for the protection of the shop workers of the country. Tt also pledges itself to further the progress of the Bill by every means that lies in its power." The Bill was supported by two thousand Warrington trade 'Unionists and would be supported .by all the trade Unionists in the country. There would be very little opposition because those who opposed the measure ware the noti-united, and numbered very few. Mr. Haves seconded and the motion was carried. Mr. TURLEY then proposed " That this meeting con- demns the unreisenable long hours worked in shops, whereby the social and moral well- being of the assis- tants is seriously affected, and their physical and intel- lectual development retarded." I Mr. Owxxseconded and the motion was agreed. to. Mr. SX \i'VElt made a statement in regard to the pro- posal to forma cricket club and expressed the opinion thata (rood team could be picked from among the mem- bers. 'About thirty were willing to join bat they had not yet been able to obtain a ground. Ithad been sug- gested that a field could be rented from another club. Mr. BIEUCHEAD said the good attendance of members at recent practice matches showed that much interest was taken in the game and he thought a club could be run successfully.

The committee which has the matter in hand was in- structed to make further inquiries as to the cost, probable subscriptions Mr. A. OwEN also reported what stvs had been taken to form a bowling club, and the secretary suggested that a joint cricket and bowling club should be established

There NTIS considerable discussion with regard to the amount of the subscription, and it Wa.B eventuttllv a c.:Troed that the two committAns should meet and docide which. would be the best course to adopt. ' _ _ _ . _

Another snug ws rendered by Mr. A. Owen and Mr. D. J. Owen .gav, a laughable stump speech.- A vote of thanks to the chairman concluded a pleasant gathering.

COMMERCIAL FAT ExitEs.—Accordina to " Kemp's Mercantile Gazette," the number of New Bankruptcy Proceedings in England and Wales gazetted during the week ending May Itith, was 77. The number in the cor- responding week of last year was 92, showing a decrease of 13, being a net decrease in 1993, to date, of 34. The War o.llce is making arrangaments with Birming- ham Water Committee for the use of the Llan Valley for the purpose of a lone range for the field guns. The proposed range is four miles long, with a danger zone .of 24 square miles.

WOMEN'S CHAT.

Princess Alice of Battenburg, who is to marry Prince Andrew of Greece, is one of the prettiest of the Princes ies in the Royal Circle. She is the daughter of Prince Louis of Battenburg. and a granddaughter of the, late Princess of Hesse. Prince Louis occupies a dis- tinguished position in the Royal Navy, and is the elder !brother of the late Prince Henry of Battenberg, so !Princess Alice, who was born at Windsor in 1885, is first cousin to the children of Princess Beatrice. Prince Andrew of Greece, who has been staying over I here, is the fourth son of the King and Queen of Greece. He was born in Athens in 1882 and is of course a nephew of Queen Alexandra. Miss Dorothy Levett, who drove her car in the long distance non-stop trial from Glasgow to London, is so pleased with her performance that she is quite ready to .do the journey over again. She has bean giving an account of her experiences on the road, from which it would appear that Miss Lovett has all the enthusiasm , which so many woman display over the motor car. She . finished thirteenth in the competition, butes an average of 18 miles an hour had to be maintained, the travelling must at times have been anything but legal and sedate. Miss Levett adm:t3 that at times • she travelled forty miles an hour, and though this sounds simple and easy, when one has a car that %ill 'go at even greater speed, yet it needs some nerve and courage to drive at such a rate. At two or three places the kindly disposed stranger warned Miss Levett that the police were on the look-out aherti, when of course, the speed was regu- lated accordingly. Not so long ago, a woman driving a motor car would have attracted as much notice as a woman 'bus driver, but though Miss Levett is the first lady to drive her own car in a 40) mile c-nupetWon, it is becoming a common thing now, to see a woman in charge of a motor. The 'mechanical vehicle does not compare favourably with the ordinary carriage for a display of womanly grace and charms, but the notion that fashion- able women would never take to the motor car for this reason, has proved quite erroneous. On the other hand the motor car has proved more effectual than any other influence in inducing women to dress With studied plainness. It is quite impossible to wear fine clothes for motoring, and therefore superfluous ornament has to be abandoned, but it is not often that appearances have to give way to convenience, in matters of women's dress.

The new motoring hat, which hails from Paris is a distinct improvement upon mech of the headgear fashioned for thisparticular pursuit, but even this latest device leaves something to be desired. In shape it is Napoleonic, and the satin straw of which it is built, is of the softest, and most pliable possible. The curtain of straw which completely covers the hair, ties under the chin by means of soft ribbon strings, and when not motoring it is possible to turn this curtain which is lined to match the strings, up over the crown, when the effect is that of a smart walking, or cycling hat. The new belts of plaited straw are!likely to be widely patronised this siumner,as they areso much less weighty and heating than the leather, suede, or metal varieties. At pre ;et these straw belts can only be had in com- parativery few tints, but doubtless it will soon be an easy matter to match almost any shade of colour. As regards shape, they arestraight all round, and about two inches in width. Almost all the latest leather belts are perforated, and .we should be careful to purchase these only. It is most necessary that ventilation should be provided for, in the matter of heather belts. Buttons form an important item in the fashionable toilette, and these range in size from the smallest pos- sible to the huge specimens - seen on long travelling or motoring coats. Dozens upon dozens of tiny velvet botton.s appear on some of the smartest cloth gowns velvet applique—matching in tone- that of the buttons exactly—finding a place about the bolero or shoulder capes. For lace dresses, lace buttons may be had, linen buttons for linen gowns, also *se covered with the modish tussore, for which there is such a considerable, rage just now. The cut steel button is out-of-date somewhat, but the ivory specimen is still in evidence also that of shining gold. Those among us in search of presents for our men folk can scarcely do Letter than fix upon the walking sticks which are now to be hid, fitted with some new and useful device. It is possible to obtain one with a curved handle, which conceals a pipe; another, a crook shape, which harbours a match box, and yet other handles which hold cigarettes,pencil cases, dog whistles, etc. Then there are the tiny electric lamps which fit into any little nook or corner of a travelling bag,or can even be slipped into one of the numerous masculine pockets. The silver pocket flask with monogram or crest inscribed thereon,is another vary desirable gift, and a fairly inexpensive one too. The Countess of Warwick only recently completed her book on" Warwick Castle and its Earls," upon which she has been engaged for several years pact. It will be published immediately by Messrs. Hutchinson, and will be without doubt the book of Ake season. As Lady Warwick observes, "the history tall the Castle is coincident with that of England" and its historic associations are interwoven with so many romantic stories that we may be sure it will he a veryfascinating volume that we shall have from the pen of this gifted authoress.

Madame Caine, to whom an overdose of accnite so nearly proved fatal, received many marks of favor from Queen Victoria and there is a bust of herself at Windsor Castle carved by the. Countess Glerchen. She has been often in this country and sung before distinguished audiences, but she does not like our climate, and the air she shys "is helvy as lead upon the chest of a French- woman. Madame Calne is a native of Southern France and this no doubt partly accounts for her dislike of our northern clin-ate. Like Sarah Bernhardt, rid some other great artistes she is not without morbid idiosyncrasies and one of these is, to have her grave and tombstone in readiness. She had the narrowest escape from com- pleting these preparations, for instead of taking a few drops of Cucture of aconite on sugar for a cold she swallowed a spoonful of it, and was for some hours hovering between life and death. The South African Expansion Society, which has been instrumental in sending out a, large number of women to the sub-Continent, has adopted the more appropriate name of the South African Colonisation Society. Since the Society began operations last year, it has sent out nearly 1300 women, for all iof whom a safe and certa ia home was found. The applicants hav:,‘. numb:wed between six and seven thousand, but thoss with suitable qualiticationa, and the highest character have been accepted, and the failures have been less than 3 per cent. Some few people have criticised the purpose and methods of the Society, but on the whole. it seems to be doing a meritorious work, in a thoroughly satis- factory manner.

MADGE.

Sports at Wilderspeol.

The Examiner, Friday, May 22, 1903. (F ASH lONS OF TO-DAY. STOCKTON HEATH PAROCHIAL aa.A2iolVr",

The short skirt which clears the ground all round, and therefore is much more convenient for street wear than a long, trailing garment, has evidently come to stay; and this is a fact hardly to be wondered when one con- siders the discomfort attendant on the former style which in fine weather acted as a sort of street-sweeper, and in wet weather not only absorbed all the attention of the Wearer •as she fruitlessly attempted to pre- serve it from contact with the muddy streets, but .proved a source of annoyance to the beholders by the exhibition of mud-spattered folds of cloth, which no efforts could save from defilement. The followers of fashion are wonderfully patient with the ,whims of Madame La Mode, but there are times when common sense asserts itself, and exaggera- tions or absurdities are thrown aside • and comfort reigns temporarily over modish- ness. I do not, however, for a moment mean to suggest that the graceful sw.vaag garments which gratify our artistic senti-

inents, and are so .pleasing therefore to behold, are no .longer to be seen in the realms of fashion. Far from that I The delicate, fragile, yet soft materials which are so beauti- ful in themselves as to excite "general admira- tion, which this season has produced, are not adapted to anything but picturesque and flowing lines and draperies, and therefore the more dressy toilettes for • afternoon or indoor wear will all be of the elaborate and trailing order.

- - The smart toilette sketched is of mouse- brown canvas, with both the short sac bolero and the skirt cut rather short. Both skirt and coat are plissé, which gives the effect of long lines of perpendicular; pleats from shoulder to feet, and in this wise prevents the shortening effect which the short skirt is apt *to produce. The. little • bolero is ren7 dered more dressy-looking by a .deep collar of fine muslin edged and appliquéd with lace. The costume is lined with mauve silk through- out, and the neat fitting deep band is of silk of the same colour. Tiny little gold buttons apparently hold the lace and, muslin collar to the bolero. The toilette. is an exceedingly stylish summer costume and wbuld look very well finished by a ,burnt straw hat trimmed with lilac flowers and leaves.

Very many of the newest coats show a fancy collar of either silk trimmed with lace or entirely of lace. There are very many styles of lace to be bought at very reasonable prices, from the wonderful imitations Of the best patterns of real lace to the coarser makes, and a plain costume can be made to look smart by the addition of a dainty _lace collar. A little lace eth:ing •at the sleeves and a chou of .soft yet 1-right silk of any of the fashionable shades arranged in front where the points of the collar meet will work wonders with a dull-coloured costume.

Some of the newest collars have long stole ends, which add to their attractiveness. The pretty lace collar might 'almost more fitly be 'called a cape as it comes nearly to the line of the waist The lace used has a deli- cate floral pattern worked on the fine net which forms the background, and it makes a dainty finish to the silk blouse worn under- neath. It can be worn for indoors or street wear throughout the entire summer, and some of the smartest French gowns owe their success to a collar of this description. There are some very charming little • brilliant brooches shaped like a button, which show to. advantage when used to fasten down the front of a lace collar or a dressy blouse. These can be had in sets, and their sphere of usefulness -is unbounded, for they are so easily and quickly transferred from one piece of dress tn enott

A SUMMER HAT.

One longs instinctively for the quick ap- proach of summer skies and sunshine in order to appear, gowned in the fascinating materials with which the shops are full. illuslins of every description, cur old friend and ally grass lawn, and coarse linens are the pre- dominating fashionable fancies, together with everything that is coarse-garined and. open- meshed. Some of these are worked with raised spots or sprigs for everything that is embroidered, and nothing that is plain is the rule in the world of dress.

The straw hat illustrated is designed for summer wear, and it is of an exceedingly pretty and smart style copied from a French model. The crov..n is of pink roses • closeiy massed together, and veiled with some fine light brown . tulle. The straw is of a burnt tone of colour, and a deep brown velvet bow rests on the hair at the back. The hat is tilted slightly over the face in front, and would therefore prove becoming to a piquant style of good looks. Another hat of the same straw had the crOwn trimmed by. cross- wise folds of pale sea-green silk, which came to a point at either side where a cluster of dark red cherries of a most natural-looking description rested. • The bow at the back was of black velvet,

COMMITTEE.

The Reprzs3nt3tio3 on the Distpict Council.

A meeting of the Stockton Heath Parochial Com- mittee was held on Thursday last week in the National Schools. Mr. Pilling presided and there were also present Messrs Fairhurst, Whitley, Clarke, Pearson, Mclntyre, Farrington (inspector), Swinton (surveyor), and Ashton (clerk).

===== 0002601_19030522_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
THE NIGHTSOIL CONTRACT.

I The Clerk reported that in accordance with instruc tions he had received at the last meeting of the com- mittee he had Written to Mr. Lunt in respect to his quarterly account, stating that the committee had de- cided to withhold the payment of the account until the work was done more satisfactorily. To this Mr. Lunt replied stating that unless an immediate payment was made he would discontinue the wort:. He (Mr. Ashton) replied to Mr. Lunt's letter advising him not to take such a step and pointing out that if he did so the committee would have. to get the work done by some- one else and would charge him with the expense. Mr. Lunt replied and pointed out that the work had been done by him for the last four months without a single Ipayment having been made and that he had expended on the work considerably more than the amount of his last quarter's account. In concluding his letter he again stated that unless an immediate payment was made he I would discontinue the work.

Mr. Farrington said he was able to report most favourably in regard to the manner in which the work was at present being done by Mr. Lunt and recom- mended that the account be passed for payment that night. He was satisfied with the way in which the work was then being done. On the motiOn of Mr. Clarke, seconded by Mr. Whitley, it was decided to pass the account for payment.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0108.txt | included=true | included =====
THE -REPRESENTATION OF THE DISTRICT COUNCIL.

Mr. Whitley, in accordance with notice of motion given at the previous meeting of the Committee, moved in the matter of the representation of Stockton Heath onthe Runcorn Rural District Council. In doing so he said he claimed that on account of the rapid growth of the district they in Stockton Heath were entitled to two representatives on the District Council. Applica- tionhad already been made for additional Parish Coun- cillors, and he thought the time had come when the district should be divided into two wards with a district councillor to represent each ward. He pointed out how otherparishes in the disirictwhich were less than Stock- ton Heath had a better representation on the District Council, and in conclusion moved "That this Parochial Committee asks the District Council to approach the County Council by order to fix or alter the number of district councillors from one to two for the Parish of Stockton Heath, and also that the said parish be divided into two wards, namely, the north east ward and the south west ward, London-road, to be the dividing line, and that the Councillors represent one ward each on the District Council.

Mr. Piling said the resolution was somewhat out of order because of the latter portion relating to the division of the parish into wards. Mr. Whitleywithdrew that portion of his resolution, which was then seconded by Mr. Pearson. The Clerk pointed out that Stockton Heath was the largest parish in the district. It was undoubtedly in sufficiently represented on the District Council, there being only one representative to a population of 3,500 Mr. Clarke said he had no objection to Mr. Whit- ley's proposal, and there was no doubt that they were under represented on the District Council. He was in- clined to think, however, that they would find it rather difficult to find a gentleman who could afford the time to attend the District Council meetings. He would suggest that if possible the township of Stockton Heath and Latchford Without be joined together with a joint representation of two.

Mr. Pearson pointed out that they would derive no benefit from such a step,as there were already too repre- sentatives for the two parishes. Mr. Whitley's motion was unanimously carried. PROPOSED URBAN AUTHORITY. QUESTIONS ASKED IW MR CLARKE.

Mr. Clarke proposed that the Rural District Council be asked to supply the committee, through their clerk with the following information: (1) in the event of an Urban Council being granted to the district of Stockton Heath what proportion of the rates now col- lected by the overseers from the district would be allocated to such authority? (2) What rates would the district still have to pay to the Runcorn Rural District Council, and for what purpose? (3) What has been the cost of maintaining the highways in Stockton Heath excepting the County Council roads during the last five years? (4). Would the money paid by the Manchester Ship Canal Company for the maintenance of that portion of Fairfield-road in Stockton Heath be handed over to the said Urban Authority. and if so what would be the amount paid? He desired, in moving the question he asked, to impress upon the com-nittee the fact that he was not at the pre- sent time advocating the formation of an urban author- ityin Stockton Heath. He did not feel that the time was ripe for such a step to be taken, but he certainly thought it was advisable and only right and proper for the general knowledge of the people in the parish that they should be in possession of the information which he asked.

It was decided that the Clerk should ask the questions as moved by Mr. Clarke.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0109.txt | included=true | included =====
Bricklayers and Walking Time.

The dispute as to walking time which. arose some weeks ago at St. Helens, was referred to arbitration, and it was agreed that Judge Shand, of the Liverpool and St. Helens County Courts should be the arbitrator The Bricklayers' Society claimed that they were entitled to walking time if the job at which they were employed MS more than half a mile from their employer's yard, or place of business. Judge- Shand said he understood his duty wflt to construe the rules as they stood, and his award is as follows :—(1), The wefrd " country " in rule 6 of the working rules means the district lying outside and beyond the boundaries of thnborough of St. Helens. (2), The operatives are not entitled to walking time within the borough of St. Helens from St. Helens employers assessed by the Corporation of St. Helens. (3), A. workman is not entitled to walking time if he voluntarily applies for work on a job." The award altogether upsets the practice for years past observed in regard to walking time, and the Bricklayers' Society will doubtless give immediate notice for a revision or re-ennstruction of the whole of the rules under which they work.

LETTERS TO THE EDITOR.

NOTICE TO CORRESPONDENTS.

Authors of letters intended for publication must mall cases send their names and addresses to the Editor as a guarantee of good faith. Matter intended for publication must be written on. one sale only of the paper and sent in, if possible,not later than Thursday. Correspondents are strongly advised to write briefly. A short letter has a much greater chance of insertion than a long one. We cannot under ordinary circumstances undertake to return the manuscripts which are not used.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0123.txt | included=true | included =====
Warringtonians and the Walking Boom.

Sir,—On reading the newspapers of late, I. have been particularly struck with the fact that the professional and business gentlemen of quite a number of towns throughout the country are organising walking- con- tests. London has already experienced one, and if re- ports may be relied upon,it has proved a distinct success. Why do not the business men of Warrington follow their example? It is generally admitted thatas a healthful outdoor exercise walking can scarcely, be improved upon and there are kw people who would not derive much benefit from a few weeks' steady training. Moreover, walking is a pastime which can be indulged in by those whose advanced age, or whose laek of physi- cal robustness prevents them" from taking part in the more vigorous forms of sport. Many delight- ful walks . can be taken around Warrington, and if the business men of this town, both young and middle-aged, would only agree to unite in a walking match, there is no reason why it should not prove just as successful as that of any other town. Warrington has always held its own in athletics, and I think I am right in saying that there are many of our 'younger men who would be quite willing to undertake the duties of arranging such a contest. —Yours &c., PEDESTRIAN.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0124.txt | included=true | included =====
The Rev. Morley Stevenson and Religious Education.

have read with pleasure the speech of tho Rev. Morley Stevenson at the opening of Padgate school on Saturday last. I note he expresses his belief that ulti- mately we shall have a complete system of the national education. Here Mr. Stevenson is a tone with Free Churchmen. He believes also that children should be, trained in the religion of their parents. Free Church- men heartil3 agree. The point of difference lies here. Religious instruction, by which Mr. Stevenson means the doctrinal formularies of his own church, not the ethical and spiritual teachings of the New Testament, he hopes and believes will be taught in the State schools at the State's expense. This can never be. To this Free Churchmen will never submit. The day that sees the inauguration of a complete and graded system of education will see the Mail abolition of all Sectarian teaching in public elementary. schools.; No comprothise is possible on this point. The day for compromise with Anglicans and Roraanists on religions teaching is past and gone. It is irrelevant to say, that there are thousands of children who receive no religious instruc- tion at all exeept what they get hi the day schools. My reply to that is, if the churches believe in them- eelves and their divinely appointed mission, they will seek to win the children for Christ and give them the needed religious instruction through their own special church agencies. If not so much the worse for the churches. Publiclysupported day schools will certainly not be used for this purpose. The great passive resist- ance movement is gaining ground in th‘) country and I am glad to know in Warington also. It is not for "nothing" that Free Churchmen are refusing to pay the "new chnrch rate." The great end in view is freeing the schools and staffs from the trouble and obligation of teaching dogmatic theology and the influence of the proselytising priest. I think t can prophesy with truth that the "flowing tide" of public opinion is with us. I hope Mr. Stevenson will neither be grieved nor disap- pointed with the issue of the fight.—l am, very truly 'ours,

JOHN FRANCIS MATTHEWS, Baptist Church, Latchford, May 15th 1903.

The "London Gazette" of Tuesday night contained the following :—Militia Infantry.-3rd Battalion the Prince of Wales's Volunteers (South Lancashire Regiment) : Gilbert Henry Bell, gentleman,to be second lieutenant, 'dated 20th May. 4th Battalion King's Liverpool Regiment, Lieutenant A. B. Stephenson to be instructor of musketry, dated 20th May. Death of a Warrington Worthy.

Many will regret to-hear that M. Thomas Turner, who was the oldest inhabitant of the town, died at his residence in Sankey Bridges, shortly before five o'clock on Tuesday morning. He was born in the year 1809, and was therefore 91 years of age. Up to the previous Thursday Mr. Turner had enjoyed remarkably good health for one who had reached such n ripe old age. but on that day he was seized with a chill whilst gardening and it resulted in his death. He was con- scious almost up to the last moment. It may be interesting to note that the deceased is the father of Mr. Frank Turner, the superintendent of the Fire Brigade, and that his grandson is the deputy-superin- tendent. Mr. Turner has left to mourn his loss four sons and one daughter. The Latter is in America, where she has resided for about 20 years. Mr. Turner had no fewer than 23 grandchildren; 13, great grand- children, and two great-great grandchildren. He dis- tinctly remembered the time whn no fewer than 90 coaches daily passed through Warrington, and when there was only one policeman. His wife died about 30 years ago, and was buried in Great Sankey-Church yard, where the remains of Mr. Turner were laid to rest yesterday. • Drowned in a Ditch. An inquest was held at the Police Station on Thurs- day evening concerning the death of James Monaghan, a threshing machine labourer, who lodged at , 16, Union-street, and who was found drowned in a ditch on Tuesday ..afternoon.—Patrick Flattery, general labourer, gave evidence of identification, and said he last saw deceased alive at 1140 on Tuesday morning in Sankey-street and he appeared to be in his usual health. Deceased was subject to fits.----Margaret Jones, wife of Robert Jones, 19, Baxter-street, stated that at four o'clock on Tuesday afternoon she was I walking along the Sankey and St. Helens Canal at Sankey, ,when sh saw the body of the deceased lying face downwards in the ditch by the side.of the towing path. She informed two men who were fishing in the canal of her discovery, and , they got the body - out of the ditch.— Albert Warburton, 25, 'Lythgoe's-lane, gave evidence I as to the recovery of the body.—Detective Barlow, coroner's officer, said he made a careful examination of the body and found no marks of violence. The ditch was at the bottom of the canal embankment and it was very probable that deceased was seized with a fit whilst walking along the towing path and fell down the embankment into the ditch.—A verdict of" Found drowned " was returned:-

THE WILD WEST SHOW.

AT WARRINGTON ON MONDAY.

The question has often been asked if the entire aggre- gation known as Buffalo Bill's Wild West and Congress of Rough Riders of the World will positively be brought here when the exhibition arrives on May 25th.We have the assurance of the management that identically the same pertormance•which delighted so many thou- sands daring th4past winter at the Olympia, London, will positively be brought here intact, not a single feature being omitted. To accomplish this gigantic task riequires an army of men and three special rail- way trains, made up of twenty-one cars each constructed oh American lines and each measuring nearly sixty feet in length.which are provided with every appliance for comfort and safety known to the railway world. Immediately after the conclusion of the night exhibition in one city, all of the horses, paraphernalia, etc.• are quickly dispatched to the goods yard where they are immediately placed upon the cars audits soon as a, train is loaded it departs immediately for the next point of wild bition, where, at break of day, the work of disembarkation commences, and in less than three hours from the time the first waggon Comes down the run everything is at the -exhibi- tion groun;s,the various teats and canopies have been erected, all the seats are in place, and everything is in readiness to receive those who desire to witness the ofternoon performance. The methods of transportation as portrayed by the Wild \Veit aggregation are in them- selves an object of great interest,and to those interested in such matters, much can be learned by witnessing the system employed in loading and unloading this wonder- ful organization, it should also be borne in mind that the weather conditions do not in any way affect the giving of a complete exhibition, and the performance will always be given in its entirety, rain or shine, upon the date and atthe hours advertised. THE BATTLE OF SAN JUAN

One of the most interesting features of the show is the military spectacle known as "The Battle of San Juan Hill." This interesting bit of realistic warfare is founded upon an epoch of the late war between the United States and Spain and the scene is laid in the island of Cuba, in whose behalf the United States government took up arms. The incident will be recalled as the one in which Colonel(now President)BoOsevelt led his famous rough riders up the strongly fortified hill and Succeeded in routing the Spaniards from what was col:- sidered to be an almost impregnable position, which practically ended the land fights in this hotly contested campaign. The spectacle is in two parts,the first showing American troops going into bivouac at night and how the soldiers enjoyed a relaxation from their long tramp by indulging in all sorts of pastimes, songs and dances, after which all is still excepting the dull .tread as the -sentries patrol their beats,but with the dawn of day the bugle call brings everyone to his feet and the lines are formed ready for the march, when scbtats return apprising the American Generals of their close proxim- ity to the Spanish firing lines. Immediately,orders are issued to prepare for action and the battle soon begins in earnest. After a short bat desperate fight the American troops succeed in gaining possessicin of the fortified hill, the Spanish General lays down his arms, and the Stars and Stripes of the United States are seen to flutter from the flag staff so recently supporting the colours of the now conquered 130114.

===== 0002601_19030522_art0131.txt | included=true | included =====
THE ROUGH RIDERS.

Unique, superb and possessing an elan peculiarly their own the Roosevelt Rough Riders really belong to the twentieth century. The detachment of fearless horsemen bearing this title who appear in the Buffalo Bill Wild West Exhibition are veterans of this wonder- ful military organization and side by side with the Cuban patriots enact the scenes which have made them famous the world over, in consequence of their faindus charge up Sin Juan Hill during the Sninish-Americae controversy in Cuba. When the United States Govern- ment took up arms against Spain in order to secure the independence of Cuba, President Theodore Roosevelt then occupied the position of Assistant Secretary. of War. Imbued with that patriotic spirit which dominates the whole Anglo-Saxon race, he resigned his post and quickly gathered around him a body of young, energetic, and fearless cow-boys, scouts and plainsmen, supple- mented by athletic recruits from all ranks of society, and every avocation of American life, who were formally christened the "First United States Volunteer Cavalry." The /American nation how- ever have a happy faculty of applying pet titles to their various regiments and someone suggested that Colonel Roosevelt's command should be known as the "Rough Riders," a name which had already become popularized by Colonel F. W. Cody in his Wild West exhibition as descriptive of the highest type of daring, vigorous and skilful riders whom he had brought together from all parts of the world in the Wild West arena. The Press and public quickly accepted the sug- gestion, and it was not long before the official com- munications of the government began to be addressed to the "Rough Riders." President Roosevelt in his book says: "For some reason or other the public promptly christened us the 'Rough Riders.' At first we 'fought against the use of the term, but to no pur- pose; and when finally thl Gaierals of Division and Brigade began to write in formal,communications about our regiment as 'Rough Riders' we adopted the title ourselves." The "Rough Riders" who appear in the Wild West arena recrnitei from the ranks of President Roosevelt's famous regiment and all were present and fought from Sibeney to Santaigo eventually climbing the slopes of San JU-111 Hill alininistering the fin LI defeat to Spanish arms in Cuba.

DEATH OF AN OLD Pi ESIDENT.

On Saturday morning last, Mr. Joseph Massey, of 300 Earle-street, Earlestown, passed away, rather unexpect- edly in his 60th year. For many years the deceased had been a Martyr to bronchitis, and because of ill-health he had to retire from active work some .five years ago. A few days before his death, however, he was taken , ill suffering from influenza, but his medical attendant pronounced the attack a mild one. On Friday night he was much worse, and on Saturday morning about nine o'clock he suddenly expired. Mr. Massey came to Earlestown front Preston about 35 years ago, and for the major portion of that time he occupied the position of foreman in the waggon building-depbt of the Viaduct Works. Failing health, however, brought about his retirement on the pension fund, and he was in receipt of a pension at the time of his death. He leaves a widow but no family.

• The funeral took place on Wednesday and was attended by a large number of workmen and foremen, and several pensioners, in addition to Mr. .1. W. Emmett, the works Superintendent and Mr. Ormand. The interment was at the cemetery, and the service was performed by the Rev. C. E. Dove, Wesleyan Minister, the deceased being one of the trustees of the Wesleyan Church.

Cycling.

There has been a welcome chaup in the weather conditions this 7veek., and cyClists have not been slow to take advantage of it. Following the steady and. continuous downpour on Saturday, and the frequent showers on Sunday, Monday was really an ideal day for the wheeler. There ,was radiant sunshine, but a light breeze tempered the heat, and the roads were in grand condition. The writer had a spin to one of Cheshire's most delightful viliages—Rostherne, which is at present looking almost at its best. Cyclists who have never visited the place should certainly make a point of doing so for they will be amply re- paid. The view from the churchyard which over- looks the placid waters of the mere. is one of the prettiest imaginable, whilst the village itself. with its quaint cottages and trim gardens is a charming spot.

Rugby Football.

The annual rnt jug of the Warrington Football Club was held on Tuesday evening, and was attended by an exceedingly large number of members. An hour sufficed to transact the business which, on the whole, was conductel amicably.. • All the old officials were re-elected, but there was one change in the constitu- tion of the committee. Ur. T. Barnes displacing Mr. S. W. Jeffery. " Lawyer "literally talked himself on again, but lam sorry he has taken the place of so . useful a man as Mr. Jeffery,

The reports were passed without dissent, and there were numerous complimentary references to the treas- urer's statement, and to Mr. Pemberton h:mself. Com- plete unanimity and heartiness characterized the passing of a resolution to mike Mr. Pemberton a testi- monial of 20 guineas in re,3ogaition of the fact that he had completed 21 years as treasurer of the Club. Some of the allusions to his worth would have mide Mr. Pemlierton blush hid he been present, but he dis- creetly retired ere this stage in the .proceedings was raached. It was also decided that the presentati9n should be made at a public meeting of the members of the Club and not in committee.

In connection with the nomination of officials for the Northern League, Mr. J. E. Warren, of Warring- ton, has formrdly notified that it is not his intention t, stand for the office of socretary. FATAL ACCIDENT IN SANKEY

STREET.

A Woman Knocked Down by a Horse.

IMr. S. Brighouse, County Coroner, held an inquiry at the Warrington Police Station on Thursday evening into the circumstances attending the death of Agnes Jones, 53 years of age, wife of Joseph Jones, it forge labourer, residing at 91 Bostock-street, who died on Tuesday a'ftemoon. • Joseph Jones,-deeeased's husband, stated that about I .8 o'clock oji Saturday evening, the 9th inst he and his ! wife went 'to town for the purpose of Cloing some shopping. Wheß opposite the Woolpaek Inn, Sankey- street, deceased stepped oil the footpath with the intention of crossing the street in order to give some money to a man who was playing a street organ. When she reached the middle of the street she was knocked down by a horse attached to a bread van. He at once went to her assistance and she was conveyed home in a cab. Dr. Liston attended her until she died at 1.43 p.m. on Tuesday. . In answer to Mr. Browne (who appeared for the driver of the vehicle) witness stated that the horse was not going very quickly. The animarshead caught deceased and caused her to fall. The van did not pass over her. The street was crowded at the time. P,C. Bennett said that at 8.40 p.m. on the date in question he was on duty in Sankey-street end on hearing a scream went to see what was the matter. He was informed that deceased had been knocked down be a horse attached to a bread van. He assisted in getting her in a cab and after seeing her home 'went for Dr. Liston. The horse and van passed him a few seconds before the accident and they were going at it very slow pace. The driver pulled up within a yard from the spot ' where deceased was knocked down. i Williams Wills, driver in the employ of Messrs. Pier- point and Company. bakers, said he was its charge of the horse and van in (pa :Mon. When deceased stw the vehicle coining she hesitated whether to cross the • street or to turn back, and the horse's nose caught her and cruised her to fall. Witness did all he could to pull up. The horse's feet did not touch deceased. Harry Walmsley, who was playing the street organ • corroborated,and said that if deceased had ,ToiWstraight on without hesitating the accident would not have occurred. The driver did all he possibly could to avoid it. • , • The Coroner said Dr. Liston had certified that when he attended deceased on the date of the accident she H. M. ASIITON's LIFE TONIC is the KiNo OF was suffering from bruises on the left hip and thigh, Tosics.-63, SANkEY-STREET, Warrington.--Advt. and also from heertdisease of longstanding, and shock. She had an attack of syncope, which was accelerated by the accident, and this was the cause of death. The jury returned a verdict of " Accidental Death " and exonerated the driver from all blame. • . THIS IS NOT A PUZZLE: WHAT IS IT? i LCRIC!(ETU (111ICKBTBRS will be glad to know that GEO. G. BUBSET Yon have two minutes to spare, haven't you? Of V Aim Co's Catalogue is out and may be obtained !row course. You are not in a hurry to catch a train, or pay Spots Dealers. This Manufacturing Firm is always bent a bill? Well, then, answer us this question: Whet on improvements. Their new Compouud Ball "POLLOID • is the leading sign of a condition of things in your body the firare- is the most perfect resemblance to the leather Match 13,l which, if not soon corrected, leads straight to, that has been iroduced, costing under half the price. For , Lealing Clubs G. G. B. & Co.'s " lex yard; and. yet none but healthy persons ever have it, and paramount. over all Cricket Balls. (61)Dup Covar 'I reigns they have it erery day? You will scfatch your head for hours before you bit upon the true answer. and yet. it is simple as simple can ' be. Come to think, you had better read this article straight through, and perhaps the truth will dawn on your mind without our being obliged to tell you outright. Let us then get at it gradually through listening to the tale of a person who once had a very unhappy bit of experience. Off and on, for twelve years, this lady suffered in- tense pain in the chest, accompanied often with a feel- ing of sinking in the pit of the stomach, at times so un- bearable that she would roll on the floor in agony. Even when free from pain, which was not often, she scarcely dared venture out of doors for fear of falling—her head wont so dizzy and her legs felt so useless. The feeling she would never get better made her terribly low and depressed. She was a wife and the mother of nine children, which made this calamity all the more serious. Dear,dear, what terrible tumbles we take sonietimes,don't we? To-day on the hill-top viewing our farm; -to- morrow asking employment from its new owner. So life goes. This lady's experience was by no means un- common, nor was it all cloud and no sunshine, as we shall presently see. But first, let us finish her story.. She felt hungry but could not eat. Her tongue was furred and there was a nasty taste ever present in her mouth. A persistent hacking cough shook her from head to toot. Though constantly under medical treatment, she experienced no benefit, until on the advice of a neigh- bour she bought a bottle of Mother Seigel's Syrup. This Hufliced to relieve the pain and perseverance in its use effected a complete cure. Writing from her home in New-street, Ridley, Rent, she says : "I feel better now tan I have done for eighteen years. I relish my food, sleep well and feel well. After twelve years of pain, Mother Seigel's Syrup cured me when everything else failed. I have a family of nine children and attribute their freedom from sickness to the fact that I occasionally give them a dose of Mother Seigel's Syrup. (Signed) (Mrs.) Sarah Banks." Now, what is it, in your body which, if not soon corrected, leads to death,—a symptom which none but healthy persons ever have, and they have it every day? Why, hunger, to be sure. Unsatisfied hunger means starration, and in the end a// lingering diseases kill by starvation. Eat and digest and you live, and arelealthy. Lose your appetite and digestion and your death-warrant is signed. Mrs. Banks' trouble began in the mere loss of appetite. When digestion and appetite fail, the wisest course is to restore tone to the stomach and blood by the use of the root and herb tonic which made Mrs. Banks feel better than she had done for eighteen years..

The CHAIRMAN : The Master has decided to stay three weeks after the termination of his notice in order that he may finish the work in hand before he leaves. (Hear, hear.) Mr. A. J. JOLLEY, in referring to the proposal to employ a number of probationer nurses, said the com- mittee were. about to take advantage -of the valuable services of the admirable officer the Board had in the superintendent nurse under whose auspices they had seen considerable reforms in the hospital—reforms that they had every reason to believe would lead to the treatment of the sick poor being materially improved and which woUld be effected by. an absolute saving in money. From the figures given in the Master's report it would be seen that the numbers in the hospital were much larger than formerly. It went to prove that a. number of poor people who used to have a great preju- dice against entering the institution were now coming in and getting ths benefit of it. That in itself was a ' great gain. With reference to the resignation of the Master and Matron, he would suggest that the House Committee be empowered by the Board to make the preliminary arrangements with a view of having three of the best candidates brought before the Board for the final appointment. Mr. KNOWLES thought it an excellent suggestion. The minutes were agreed to, and it was decided that the Board should refer to the House Committee the examination of the applications and the selection of candidates as well as the visitation of any of the Work- houses that might be thought desirableat which favour- al le applicants might be employed. The final selection will rest with the Board at the next monthly meeting. . ANOTHER LOAN. G. 20, RENSHAW STREET, LIVERPOOL, Pilling, (vice-chairman), S. Wright, Admiral Parry, J. (2 Minutes from Central stna Lime Street stationa.) Mclntyre, J. Stokes, A. U. Guest, J. Knowles, J, T. Collier, J. Spruce, R. Billingaon, T. Darlington, J. B. Chadwick, S. Clarke,q. Edwards, T. Lewis, J. Baggaley P, Gleave, NV. A, Wallington, J. Bolsha.w, NV. H. Brad- ford, T. Ellison, with Messrs. G. T. Ashton (clerk), W. E. Hough, (assistant clerk), Dr. Adams (medical officer), W. Swinton, and J. Diggle (surveyors) J. Farrington (inspector). STOCKTON HEATH'S REPRESENTATION. Arising out. of the Stockton Heath minutes was a request that the District Council would allow the dis- trict two representatives instead of one, and that the Parish Ciuncil be furnished with certain financial par- ticulars. Mr. Pilling, in moving the adoption of the minutes, said that Stockton Heath was not fully represented according to its rateable value. The idea of asking for certain financial information was not from any wish to form an urban district, but simply to satisfy the rate- payers. It was resolved that the information be given to the Parish Council, and that a small committee consisting of Messrs. Woodward, Knowles, Stokes, Darlington,and Pilling be appointed to consider the advisability of petitioning the County Council to increase the repre- sentation. It was further resolved that Mr. Farrington, the nuisance inspector, attend the village weekly to look after certain sanitary matters. THE WARRINGTON LIGHT RAILWAY. The Board of Trade enclosed copies of the Warring- ton Corporation and Warrington and Northwich Light, Railway Orders in the form in which they will be con- firmed. The Clerk said there was practically no alteration to the Order save the insertion of one clause about the lighting of dangerous parts of the line. THE POLLUTION OF CERTAIN STREAMS. • Under date April 23, the Clerk of the County Council, asked on behalf of the Rivers Pollution Committee, what further progress had been made by the Rural District Council to prevent the pollution complained of in this district. The Clerk said he had, already furnished Mr. Potts with the present position of affairs. THE PROPOSED PURCHASE OF RUNCORN MAINS. Mr, E. Marshall, Cleric to the Runcorn Urban Council wrote on April 29, with regard to the Halton and Weston Water Supply. He said "At the Water Com- mittee's meeting held to-day your letter was read and I am desired to say that a view of the fact that the memorial to the County Council with regard to the pro- posed extension of boundaries has been lodged and that if an inquiry is held it will probably be at an early date, The Water Committee do not propose going into the matter of the sale of mains, apparatus (sr.c., for the present. My committee think that you will see that the attitude taken by them is a reasonable one under the circumstances." The Clerk said it was not worth while to press for an arbitrator at the present time seeing that the 'whole question would probably come within a short period, under the notice of the County Council. THE FENCING OF COMMON PIT. , Mr, R. Dobson, coroner for the Honour and Fee of Halton, wrote suggesting that the Common Pit at Halton in which a child was recently drowned should be fenced. He had corresponded with the Runcorn Urban Council and had reason to believe they would fence theft adjoining the recreation ground. Major Orred's agent replying to the Coroner said fencing was all right round the pit. If the Runcorn authorities cared to fill up a portion of the pit with proper materials he did not think Major Orred would object provided they did not interfere with the water suppf, in any way. Mr.Eliggle, surveyor; said he had inspectei the place and found than so far as the .Rural Council were con- cerned the fencing was quite satisfactory. Resolved that Messrs. Guest and Chad Wick visit the place and report at the next meeting whether anything ought to be done. A DANGEROUS FOOTPATH AT LATCHFORD LOCKS. Mr. J.C. Parr, Chairman to the Grappenhall Parish Council, wrote drawing attention to the dangerous and unprotected state of the footway across the Latchford Locks of the Ship Canal. The Parish Council were of opinion that the matter should be pressed very strongly in view of the loss of life which has already happened, and the many accidents and immersions which have already occurred. The Ship Canal should be obliged to protect the public footpath in accordance with the stipulation of the Act, especially as the population in the district was likely to increase. It was decided that a Committee, consistinc,* of Messrs. Piling, Admiral Parry., Knowles, and Bradford, wait on the Manchester Ship Canal Company to con- sider the matter. THE EXTENSION SCHEME. • The following letter was received from Mr. J. B. Houghton Chairman of the Halton Parish Council. The Parish Council of Halton desire to call the attention of the Runcorn Rural District Council to a memorial presented by the Urban District Council of Runcorn, to the Cheshire County Council with reference to proposed extension of boundaries and to express their strong dis- approval to be separated from the Rural District Council atea, and to request the Rural District Council to oppose the petition as strongly as possible. At a largely attended meeting of the Weston ratepayers held on the 28th ultimo in the Parochal schools it was re- solved unarrimouslythat the stongest possible resistance be.given to the proposedamalgamation with Runcorn. Referring to the Same question Mr. James Collier clerk to the Weston Parish Council wrote as follows : At a special meeting of my Council held at the Church Schoolroom, Weston, I was instructed to forward you a copy of the following resolution which was passed un- animously :—The Parish Council of Weston desire to call the attention of the Runcorn Rural District Coun- cil to the petition presented by the Urban Council to the Cheshire County Council with regard to the proposed extension of the boundaries and to express their strong opposition to be separated from Runcorn Rural District Council to oppose the petition as strongly as possible. The Chairman said at was a most important matter for the Rural Council. The Clerk said nothing had yet been heard from the County Council but the probability was that an inquiry would be held. He recommended that a small com- mittee be appointed to take necessary steps to oppose the Urban Council's petition. It was resolved that Messrs. Stokes, B ixter, Linaker, Williams, Wright, Collier, Woodward, Pilling, Guest, and Chadwick be appointed, to engage counsel to oppose the petition with power to act without reference to the Rural Council.
